Geometric bias in eigenspace perturbation under random heterogeneous noise
Fengkai Liu, Ke Wang, Wanjie Wang

Abstract
Spectral methods rely fundamentally on the stability of principal eigenspaces under random perturbations. Classically, this stability is quantified by the Davis-Kahan and Wedin theorems, which bound the eigenspace error using the operator norm of the noise and the relevant spectral gaps. While these worst-case bounds are sharp for arbitrary deterministic perturbations, they can be wasteful in the low-rank signal-plus-random-noise setting, as they fail to capture the fine-grained interaction between the signal geometry and the noise distribution. In this paper, we study the spectral perturbation of signal-plus-noise matrices corrupted by sparse, random noise with an arbitrary, inhomogeneous variance profile. We demonstrate that under heterogeneous noise variances, the empirical eigenvectors suffer a systematic, deterministic geometric bias that is entirely invisible to classical perturbation bounds. By leveraging the Quadratic Vector Equation (QVE) and establishing fine-grained isotropic local laws, we derive near-optimal, non-asymptotic perturbation bounds for the leading eigenspaces in the operator and 2 → ∞ 2\to\infty norms. The bounds separate the usual signal-to-noise contribution, stochastic fluctuations, and structured geometric bias terms determined by the alignment between the signal eigenspaces and the row-wise variance profile.



1
Introduction

Spectral methods are a basic tool for extracting low-dimensional structure from high-dimensional noisy data. In many statistical and network models, the observed matrix consists of a low-rank signal corrupted by random noise with independent but non-identically distributed entries. Classical perturbation theory, including Weyl’s inequality and the Davis-Kahan-Wedin
\sin\Theta
theorem, bounds the error in empirical eigenspaces using the operator norm of the noise and the spectral gaps. However, when the signal is low-rank and the perturbation comes from random noise, these worst-case deterministic bounds are inherently conservative. While recent work has developed refined bounds that exploit stochastic structure (discussed further in Section
4
), we identify a key feature of heterogeneous random noise: the variance profile creates a systematic geometric bias in the empirical eigenvectors. Even when signal directions are orthogonal in the standard inner product, they may not be orthogonal with respect to the variance-weighted inner product induced by the noise. This paper develops non-asymptotic eigenspace perturbation bounds that separate three distinct contributions: the standard signal-to-noise ratio, the random fluctuations controlled by isotropic local laws, and the deterministic bias induced by the variance profile.

Our model is

\widetilde{A}=A+E,

(1.1)

where
A\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
is a deterministic matrix with rank
r
, representing the signal, and
E
is the random noise. Denote the eigen-decomposition of
A
as
A=U\Lambda U^{\mathrm{T}}=\sum_{i=1}^{r}\lambda_{i}u_{i}u_{i}^{\mathrm{T}}
where the non-zero eigenvalues of
A
are ordered algebraically:

\lambda_{1}\geq\dots\geq\lambda_{r}.

The ordered eigenvalues and their corresponding eigenvectors of
\widetilde{A}
are denoted as
\widetilde{\lambda}_{i},\widetilde{u}_{i}
for
1\leq i\leq n
.

Let

r_{+}:=\#\{j:\lambda_{j}>0\},\qquad r_{-}:=r-r_{+},

so that

\lambda_{1}\geq\cdots\geq\lambda_{r_{+}}>0>\lambda_{r_{+}+1}\geq\cdots\geq\lambda_{r}.

In the main text we focus on the right-edge outliers and the top-
k
(
1\leq k\leq r_{+}
) positive signal eigenspace and its perturbed counterpart

\displaystyle U_{k}:=(u_{1},\dots,u_{k}),

\displaystyle\widetilde{U}_{k}:=(\widetilde{u}_{1},\dots,\widetilde{u}_{k}).

(1.2)

The left-edge (negative-spike) case is entirely analogous and follows by applying the same argument to
-A
(see Remark
2.1
). Denote
\Lambda_{k}=\mathrm{diag}(\lambda_{1},\dots,\lambda_{k})
and define
\widetilde{\Lambda}_{k}
analogously. For any index set
J\subseteq[r]
,
U_{J}
denotes the submatrix of
U
with columns indexed by
J
.

We impose the following assumptions on random noise
E
:

A
SSUMPTION
1.1

(Structural assumption on the noise)
.

Let
E
be an
n\times n
symmetric random matrix with independent centered entries (up to symmetry) and variance profile
\Sigma=(\sigma_{ij}^{2})_{1\leq i,j\leq n}
where
\mathbb{E}(E_{ij}^{2})=\sigma_{ij}^{2}.
Assume there exists a parameter
K\geq 1
such that the sub-Gaussian norm
1
1

1

The sub-Gaussian norm is of a random variable
X
is defined as
\|X\|_{\psi_{2}}:=\inf\{t>0:\mathbb{E}(\exp(X^{2}/t^{2}))\leq 2\}.
In particular,
\mathbb{P}(|X|\geq t)\leq 2\exp(-t^{2}/\|X\|_{\psi_{2}}^{2})
. See
[
78
]
for details.
of each entry satisfies:

\|E_{ij}\|_{\psi_{2}}\leq K\sigma_{ij}.

Equivalently, we can write
E_{ij}=\sigma_{ij}\xi_{ij}
where the random variables
\xi_{ij}
have mean 0, variance 1, and satisfy

\max_{i,j}\|\xi_{ij}\|_{\psi_{2}}\leq K.

Let
\sigma_{\max}:=\max_{i,j}\sigma_{ij}>0
. We further assume that there exists a parameter
\beta\geq 1
such that

\mathbb{E}|E_{ij}|^{3}\leq\beta K\sigma_{\max}\,\sigma_{ij}^{2},\qquad\mathbb{E}|E_{ij}|^{4}\leq\beta^{2}K^{2}\sigma_{\max}^{2}\,\sigma_{ij}^{2}.

(1.3)

R
EMARK
1.1

(The role of
\beta
)
.

The parameter
\beta
is introduced to prevent artificial logarithmic losses in sparse regimes by decoupling the moment controls from the sub-Gaussian norm
K
. Relying solely on
K
, a standard truncation argument yields:

\mathbb{E}|E_{ij}|^{3}\leq C\sqrt{\log(eK)}K\sigma_{\max}\sigma_{ij}^{2},\qquad\mathbb{E}|E_{ij}|^{4}\leq C\log(eK)K^{2}\sigma_{\max}^{2}\sigma_{ij}^{2},

which corresponds to taking
\beta\asymp\sqrt{\log(eK)}
. For a sparse centered Bernoulli(
p
) variable
\xi
,
K\lesssim p^{-1/2}
. This crude bound introduces a penalty of
\beta\asymp\sqrt{\log(1/p)}
. However, direct calculation shows
\mathbb{E}|\xi|^{3},\mathbb{E}|\xi|^{4}\leq\sigma^{2}
. The condition (
1.3
) thus holds with
\beta=O(1)
, avoiding the unnecessary logarithmic loss.

Although the parameter
\beta
in (
1.3
) is allowed to depend on
n
, it should be understood as an
effective moment parameter, chosen as small as possible. Under the
sub-Gaussian assumption alone, the standard truncation argument shows that one
may always take
\beta\lesssim\sqrt{\log(eK)}
.

The spectral behavior of the noise matrix
E
is governed by the maximum row-wise variance
R_{\max}:=\max_{i}\sum_{j}\sigma_{ij}^{2}
and the maximum entry-wise variance
\sigma_{\max}^{2}:=\max_{i,j}\sigma_{ij}^{2}
. To capture the heterogeneity of the noise profile, we also define the
variance oscillation
parameter:

\mathrm{osc}(\mathcal{R})=R_{\max}-R_{\min},

where
R_{\min}:=\min_{i}\sum_{j}\sigma_{ij}^{2}
. While
R_{\max}
and
\sigma_{\max}^{2}
control the standard operator norm bounds,
\mathrm{osc}(\mathcal{R})
specifically quantifies the geometric bias on the eigenvectors. For homogeneous or regular networks where
\mathrm{osc}(\mathcal{R})\approx 0
, this deterministic bias vanishes.

Let us denote
R_{i}:=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\sigma_{ij}^{2}
. Define the diagonal matrix

\mathcal{R}:=\mathrm{diag}(R_{1},\dots,R_{n})

and the
geometric bias matrix

\displaystyle\mathcal{V}:=U^{\mathrm{T}}\mathcal{R}U=(u_{i}^{\mathrm{T}}\mathcal{R}u_{j})_{1\leq i,j\leq r}.

(1.4)

The diagonal term
\mathcal{V}_{ii}=u_{i}^{\mathrm{T}}\mathcal{R}u_{i}=\sum_{k}u_{i}(k)^{2}R_{k}
records the effective variance seen by
u_{i}
. The cross term
\mathcal{V}_{ij}=u_{i}^{\mathrm{T}}\mathcal{R}u_{j}=\sum_{k}u_{i}(k)u_{j}(k)R_{k}
captures the geometric bias. Even
u_{i},u_{j}
are orthogonal, they are generally not orthogonal with respect to the inner product induced by the heterogeneous noise weights
\mathcal{R}
.

A
SSUMPTION
1.2

(General sub-Gaussian regime)
.

Fix
D>0
, we assume

\displaystyle\sqrt{R_{\max}}\geq C_{0}(D+8)K\sigma_{\max}{\log n}\quad\text{and}\quad\lambda_{1}\leq R_{\max}^{3}

(1.5)

for a large absolute constant
C_{0}>0
.

For specific noise distributions, we can weaken the first condition in Assumption
1.2
:

A
SSUMPTION
1.3

(Bounded sparse-entry regime)
.

Fix
D>0
. Suppose that there exist absolute constants
c_{0},\beta_{0}>0
such that

\max_{i,j}|E_{ij}|\leq c_{0}K\sigma_{\max}\quad\text{almost surely}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\beta\leq\beta_{0}.

In this regime, we assume only

\displaystyle\sqrt{R_{\max}}\geq C_{0}(D+8)K\sigma_{\max}\sqrt{\log n}\quad\text{and}\quad\lambda_{1}\leq R_{\max}^{3}

(1.6)

for a large absolute constant
C_{0}>0
.

R
EMARK
1.2

(On the auxiliary Assumptions
1.2
and
1.3
)
.

The first condition in both assumptions prevents any single maximal entry from dominating its row sum. Under this condition, our estimate (see Lemma
5.4
) yields
\|E\|\leq 3\sqrt{R_{\max}}
with high probability, thereby guaranteeing a macroscopic signal-to-noise separation (
\lambda_{k}\geq 3\|E\|
) for any supercritical spike
\lambda_{k}\geq 9\sqrt{R_{\max}}
.

For Assumption
1.2
, the condition
\sqrt{R_{\max}}\geq C_{0}K\sigma_{\max}\log n
is established using the sharp matrix concentration bounds of Bandeira and van Handel
[
14
]
combined with a standard truncation argument (see Lemma
5.4
). By contrast, Assumption
1.3
relaxes this sparsity requirement to
\sqrt{R_{\max}}\geq C_{0}K\sigma_{\max}\sqrt{\log n}
. This improved rate follows directly from
[
14
, Corollary 3.2]
and Talagrand’s concentration inequality. The weaker condition
\sqrt{R_{\max}}\gtrsim K\sigma_{\max}\sqrt{\log n}
in Assumption
1.3

is also available for real symmetric sub-Gaussian entries by the recent sharp matrix
concentration results
[
28
, Theorem 1.6]
.

Both assumptions naturally capture sparse regimes. For example, in a stochastic
block model with edge probabilities of order
p
, one has

R_{\max}\asymp np
,
\sigma_{\max}\asymp\sqrt{p},
and for centered Bernoulli noise,

K\lesssim p^{-1/2}.

Under Assumption
1.2
, the condition becomes

p\gtrsim\log^{2}n/n
. Under the bounded sparse-entry regime in
Assumption
1.3
, it improves to

p\gtrsim\log n/n
.

The second condition
\lambda_{1}\leq R_{\max}^{3}
excludes ultra-strong signals and is imposed simply for technical convenience. When a population spike is massive, i.e.,
\lambda_{k}\geq R_{\max}^{3}\asymp\|E\|^{6}
, its empirical counterpart
\widetilde{\lambda}_{k}
exhibits negligible fluctuations, and the Green’s function
G(z)=(zI-E)^{-1}
near
z\approx\widetilde{\lambda}_{k}
is dominated by
z^{-1}I
. Eigenvector perturbations in this regime follow directly from Neumann series expansions. Since this requires different, simpler arguments and is not our main focus, we exclude it; see
[
83
]
for a treatment without this upper bound.

For technical convenience, we assume the polynomial regime

\displaystyle R_{\max}\leq n^{100}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\sigma_{\max}^{-1}\leq n^{100}.

The exponent 100 is arbitrary and can be replaced by any sufficiently large
fixed constant. This condition is an extremely mild requirement that covers essentially all statistical models of interest. For example, in random graph models with bounded edge weights, we have
R_{\max}\leq n
. In heavy-tailed matrix models, entries are typically truncated at level
n^{c}
and
R_{\max}\leq n^{2c+1}
.

Define the
spectral gap

\displaystyle\delta_{k}:=\begin{cases}\lambda_{k}-\lambda_{k+1},&\text{ if }k<r_{+};\\
\lambda_{k}-3\sqrt{R_{\max}},&\text{ if }k=r_{+}.\end{cases}

(1.7)

With the convention that
\delta_{0}:=+\infty
.

We first state two simplified consequences of our main theorem. These
statements are intended to display the dominant scales without the full
block notation. Theorem
1
concerns an individual
outlier eigenvalue and eigenvector, while
Theorem
2
concerns the top-
k
positive
eigenspace. The precise non-asymptotic result,
Theorem
3
in Section
2
, keeps the
structured variance-profile couplings between different signal eigenspaces.

We use the standard principal angle notation. For two matrices
U,V\in\mathbb{R}^{n\times k}
with orthonormal columns, the principal angles
\theta_{1},\ldots,\theta_{k}\in[0,\pi/2]
are defined by
\sigma_{i}(U^{\mathrm{T}}V)=\cos\theta_{i}
. We write

\sin\angle(U,V)=\mathrm{diag}(\sin\theta_{1},\ldots,\sin\theta_{k}),

so that

\|\sin\angle(U,V)\|=\|(I-UU^{\mathrm{T}})V\|.

When discussing subspaces, we identify
U
and
V
with their column spaces. For
k=1
, this reduces to the usual sine of the angle between two vectors. See
[
83
]
for further discussion.

T
HEOREM
1

(Individual eigenvalue and eigenvector perturbations: simplified version)
.

Fix
D>0
. Under Assumptions
1.1
and
1.2
, suppose that
\lambda_{k}
with
k\in[r_{+}]
is a simple eigenvalue satisfying

\lambda_{k}\geq 9\sqrt{R_{\max}}

and

\displaystyle\delta_{k}^{*}:=\min\{\delta_{k-1},\delta_{k}\}\gtrsim\sqrt{k}\left(r\beta K\sigma_{\max}(\log n)^{2}+\frac{\mathrm{osc}(\mathcal{R})}{\lambda_{k}}\right),

then the following holds with probability at least
1-n^{-D}
,

\left|\widetilde{\lambda}_{k}-\lambda_{k}-\frac{\mathcal{V}_{kk}}{\lambda_{k}}\right|\lesssim r\beta K\sigma_{\max}(\log n)^{2}+\frac{\mathrm{osc}(\mathcal{R})}{\lambda_{k}},

and

\displaystyle|\sin\angle(u_{k},\widetilde{u}_{k})|

\displaystyle\lesssim\sqrt{k}\frac{\mathrm{osc}(\mathcal{R})}{\delta_{k}^{*}\lambda_{k}}+\sqrt{k}\frac{r\beta K\sigma_{\max}(\log n)^{2}}{\delta_{k}^{*}}+\frac{\sqrt{R_{\max}}}{\lambda_{k}},

\displaystyle\min_{\mathtt{s}\in\{\pm 1\}}\|\widetilde{u}_{k}-\mathtt{s}u_{k}\|_{\infty}

\displaystyle\lesssim\sqrt{k}\|U\|_{2,\infty}\left(\frac{\mathrm{osc}(\mathcal{R})}{\delta_{k}^{*}\lambda_{k}}+\frac{R_{\max}}{\lambda_{k}^{2}}+\frac{r\beta K\sigma_{\max}(\log n)^{2}}{\delta_{k}^{*}}\right)

\displaystyle+\sqrt{k}\frac{r\beta K\sigma_{\max}(\log n)^{2}}{\lambda_{k}}.

T
HEOREM
2

(Top-
k
eigenspace perturbations: simplified version)
.

Fix
D>0
. Under Assumptions
1.1
and
1.2
, suppose that
\lambda_{k}
with
k\in[r_{+}]
satisfies
\lambda_{k}\geq 9\sqrt{R_{\max}}

and

\displaystyle\delta_{k}\gtrsim\sqrt{k}\left(r\beta K\sigma_{\max}(\log n)^{2}+\frac{\mathrm{osc}(\mathcal{R})}{\lambda_{k}}\right),

then the following holds with probability at least
1-n^{-D}
:

(i)
For the operator norm bound,

\displaystyle\|\sin\angle(U_{k},\widetilde{U}_{k})\|\lesssim\sqrt{k}\frac{\mathrm{osc}(\mathcal{R})}{\delta_{k}\lambda_{k}}+\sqrt{k}\frac{r\beta K\sigma_{\max}(\log n)^{2}}{\delta_{k}}+\frac{\sqrt{R_{\max}}}{\lambda_{k}}.

(ii)
For the
2\to\infty
norm bound,

\displaystyle\min_{O\in\mathbb{O}(k)}\|\widetilde{U}_{k}-U_{k}O\|_{2,\infty}\lesssim

\displaystyle\sqrt{k}\|U\|_{2,\infty}\left(\frac{\mathrm{osc}(\mathcal{R})}{\delta_{k}\lambda_{k}}+\frac{r\beta K\sigma_{\max}(\log n)^{2}}{\delta_{k}}+\frac{R_{\max}}{\lambda_{k}^{2}}\right)

(1.8)

\displaystyle+\sqrt{k}\frac{r\beta K\sigma_{\max}(\log n)^{2}}{\lambda_{k}}.

(1.9)

Under the bounded sparse-entry regime in Assumption 1.3, the same simplified
bounds hold with
r\beta K\sigma_{\max}(\log n)^{2}
replaced by

(c_{0}+\beta_{0})rK\sigma_{\max}\log n
, up to absolute constants.

Roadmap.
The paper is organized as follows. Section
2
presents our main perturbation theorem with structured geometric couplings and both operator-norm and rowwise error bounds. Section
3
explains the geometric bias mechanism through a rank-two example and the QVE expansion, including numerical experiments and an oracle debiasing procedure. Section
4
reviews related literature. The technical development is presented in Sections
5
–
9
: we establish the QVE expansion that generates the variance-profile bias (Section
5
), state the probabilistic inputs including the isotropic local law and outlier eigenvalue locations (Section
6
), prove the main perturbation theorem using blockwise eigenvector equations (Section
7
), and establish the local law and outlier eigenvalue results (Sections
8
–
9
). Additional technical results appear in the appendices.

Notation.
For a vector
v=(v_{1},\dots,v_{n})^{\mathrm{T}}\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
, we let
\|v\|
denote its Euclidean
\ell_{2}
norm and
\|v\|_{\infty}:=\max_{i}|v_{i}|
denote its maximum absolute entry. The oscillation of a vector
v
is denoted by
\mathrm{osc}(v):=\max_{i}v_{i}-\min_{i}v_{i}
. For a matrix
B
, we define its off-diagonal part as
\mathrm{Off}(B):=B-\mathrm{diag}(B)
, where
\mathrm{diag}(B)
is the diagonal matrix formed by the diagonal entries of
B
. We use various matrix norms throughout the paper. Let
\|B\|
denote the operator norm, and let
\|B\|_{F}
denote the Frobenius norm. The
2\to\infty
norm of a matrix is defined as
\|B\|_{2,\infty}:=\max_{i}\|e_{i}^{\mathrm{T}}B\|
, which is the maximum Euclidean norm of the rows of
B
. The infinity operator norm is denoted by
\|B\|_{\infty\to\infty}:=\max_{i}\sum_{j}|B_{ij}|
. We let
\mathbb{O}(k)
denote the set of
k\times k
orthogonal matrices. For two non-negative sequences
X_{n}
and
Y_{n}
, we write
X_{n}\lesssim Y_{n}
(or
Y_{n}\gtrsim X_{n}
) if there exists an absolute constant
c>0
such that
X_{n}\leq cY_{n}
for all sufficiently large
n
. We write
X_{n}\asymp Y_{n}
if both
X_{n}\lesssim Y_{n}
and
X_{n}\gtrsim Y_{n}
hold. Throughout the paper,
C,C^{\prime}
denote positive absolute constants whose exact values may change from line to line.

2
Main perturbation results

We introduce the detailed non-asymptotic perturbation results for the top-
k
eigenspaces. Throughout the main results, we fix a parameter
D>0
. Under the general sub-Gaussian regime (Assumption
1.2
), we define

\displaystyle M_{D}^{\mathrm{gen}}:=C_{\mathrm{gen}}(D+6)^{3/2}rK\sigma_{\max}(\log n)^{2},

where
C_{\mathrm{gen}}>0
is a sufficiently large absolute constant. In the bounded sparse-entry regime (Assumption
1.3
), we can improve
M_{D}^{\mathrm{gen}}
to

M_{D}^{\mathrm{bd}}:=C_{\mathrm{bd}}(c_{0}+\beta_{0})(D+6)r\beta K\sigma_{\max}\log n.

Here,
C_{\mathrm{bd}}>0
is a sufficiently large absolute constant.

For simplicity, we denote

\displaystyle M\equiv M_{D}:=\begin{cases}M_{D}^{\mathrm{gen}},&\text{under Assumption~\ref{assump:extra}},\\
M_{D}^{\mathrm{bd}},&\text{under Assumption~\ref{assump:extra-weaker}}.\end{cases}

(2.1)

Furthermore, for fixed
k\in[r_{+}]
, define index sets

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}:=\{k+1,\dots,r_{+}\},\quad\mathcal{I}:=[r]\setminus\mathcal{J},

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}:=\{r_{+}+1,\dots,r\},\quad\mathcal{K}:=[r]\setminus\mathcal{N}.

and denote

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{k}:=\frac{10\sqrt{k}}{\delta_{k}\lambda_{k}}\left(\|\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{J}\mathcal{I}}\|+8\frac{R_{\max}}{\lambda_{k}^{2}}\mathrm{osc}(\mathcal{R})\right)+4\sqrt{k}\frac{\|\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{N}\mathcal{K}}\|}{\lambda_{k}^{2}}

(2.2)

where
\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{J}\mathcal{I}}=U_{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathrm{T}}\mathcal{R}{U}_{\mathcal{I}}
and
\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{N}\mathcal{K}}=U_{\mathcal{N}}^{\mathrm{T}}\mathcal{R}{U}_{\mathcal{K}}
. If
k=r_{+}
(thus
\mathcal{J}=\emptyset
), then
\|\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{J}\mathcal{I}}\|=0
.
We also set

\rho_{k}\equiv\rho_{k,D}:=10M_{D}+15\frac{\mathrm{osc}(\mathcal{R})}{\lambda_{k}}.

T
HEOREM
3

(Top-
k
eigenspace perturbations)
.

Fix
D>0
. Under Assumption
1.1
and one of Assumptions
1.2
and
1.3
, for any
k\in[r_{+}]
such that
\lambda_{k}\geq 9\sqrt{R_{\max}}+100\rho_{k,D}
and

\displaystyle\delta_{k}\geq\sqrt{k}\left(65\rho_{k,D}+20\frac{\mathrm{osc}(\mathcal{R})}{\lambda_{k}}\right),

(2.3)

we have the following holds with probability at least
1-n^{-D}
:

(i)
For the operator norm bound, we have

\displaystyle\|\sin\angle(U_{k},\widetilde{U}_{k})\|\leq\mathcal{B}_{k}+10\sqrt{k}\frac{M_{D}}{\delta_{k}}+2\frac{\|E\|}{\lambda_{k}}.

(ii)
For the
2\to\infty
norm bound, we first have

\displaystyle\|\widetilde{U}_{k}-P_{U_{k}}\widetilde{U}_{k}\|_{2,\infty}\leq\|U\|_{2,\infty}\left(\mathcal{B}_{k}+10\sqrt{k}\frac{M_{D}}{\delta_{k}}+8\sqrt{k}\frac{{R}_{\max}}{\lambda_{k}^{2}}\right)+15\sqrt{k}\frac{M_{D}}{\lambda_{k}}.

(2.4)

Consequently,

\displaystyle\min_{O\in\mathbb{O}(k)}\|\widetilde{U}_{k}-U_{k}O\|_{2,\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\|\widetilde{U}_{k}-P_{U_{k}}\widetilde{U}_{k}\|_{2,\infty}+\|U_{k}\|_{2,\infty}\|\sin\angle(U_{k},\widetilde{U}_{k})\|^{2},

(2.5)

\displaystyle\leq 4\|U\|_{2,\infty}\left(\mathcal{B}_{k}+20\sqrt{k}\frac{M_{D}}{\delta_{k}}+30\sqrt{k}\frac{R_{\max}}{\lambda_{k}^{2}}\right)+120\sqrt{k}\frac{M_{D}}{\lambda_{k}}.

(2.6)

The detailed bounds in Theorem
3
retain a geometric bias term
\mathcal{B}_{k}
defined in (
2.2
). In particular, it retains the quantities

\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{J}\mathcal{I}}=U_{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathrm{T}}\mathcal{R}\,U_{\mathcal{I}},\qquad\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{N}\mathcal{K}}=U_{\mathcal{N}}^{\mathrm{T}}\mathcal{R}\,U_{\mathcal{K}},

which encode how the signal eigenspaces align with the row-variance profile
\mathcal{R}=\mathrm{diag}(R_{i})
. These quantities vanish in the homogeneous case when
\mathcal{R}=cI
. More generally, by orthogonality,

\|\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{J}\mathcal{I}}\|=\|U_{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathrm{T}}(\mathcal{R}-cI)\,U_{\mathcal{I}}\|\leq\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{osc}(\mathcal{R})

by choosing
c=\frac{1}{2}(R_{\max}+R_{\min})
. Likewise,
\|\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{N}\mathcal{K}}\|\leq\frac{1}{2}\mathrm{osc}(\mathcal{R})
. Therefore, the simplified theorems in the Introduction follow directly from Theorem
3
. Indeed, the simplified dependence on
\mathrm{osc}(\mathcal{R})
in Theorem
2
should therefore be viewed as a worst-case bound for these couplings

\|\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{J}\mathcal{I}}\|,\|\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{N}\mathcal{K}}\|.

R
EMARK
2.1

(Bounds for general eigenspaces)
.

Theorem
3
is stated for the top positive eigenspace
only to simplify notation. The proof extends directly to general eigenspaces
U_{l:s}=(u_{l},\dots,u_{s})
for
1\leq l<s\leq r
. We also provide a simple alternative approach using the bounds for top eigenspaces.

For negative population spikes, we apply our framework to
-A
and
-(A+E)
. In our ordering
\lambda_{1}\geq\dots\geq\lambda_{r_{+}}>0>\lambda_{r_{+}+1}\geq...\geq\lambda_{r}
, the eigenspaces corresponding to the most negative eigenvalues become the top eigenspaces. This symmetry yields the same perturbation guarantees for the bottom eigenspaces without additional analysis.

For intermediate blocks
U_{l:s}
and
\widetilde{U}_{l:s}
where
1\leq l<s\leq r_{+}
. We use the decomposition
P_{U_{l:s}}=P_{U_{s}}-P_{U_{l-1}}
to obtain

\displaystyle\|\sin\angle(U_{l:s},\widetilde{U}_{l:s})\|\leq\|\sin\angle(U_{l-1},\widetilde{U}_{l-1})\|+\|\sin\angle(U_{s},\widetilde{U}_{s})\|,

(2.7)

and

\displaystyle\min_{O}\|\widetilde{U}_{l:s}-U_{l:s}O\|_{2,\infty}\leq\|(I-P_{U_{l:s}})\widetilde{U}_{l:s}\|_{2,\infty}+\|U_{l:s}\|_{2,\infty}\|\sin\angle(U_{l:s},\widetilde{U}_{l:s})\|^{2},

(2.8)

where

\|(I-P_{U_{l:s}})\widetilde{U}_{l:s}\|_{2,\infty}\leq\|(I-P_{U_{s}})\widetilde{U}_{s}\|_{2,\infty}+\|U_{l-1}\|_{2,\infty}\|\sin\angle(U_{l-1},\widetilde{U}_{l-1})\|.

Thus, the perturbation bounds for
U_{l:s},\widetilde{U}_{l:s}
follow by substituting the estimates for the top-
s
and top-
(l-1)
eigenspaces. By symmetry, analogous decompositions hold for intermediate blocks of negative spikes. We prove (
2.7
) and (
2.8
) in Appendix
F
.

Finally, we formulate a perturbation result for the entire eigenspace
U=(u_{1},\dots,u_{r})
. Denote

\widetilde{U}_{\mathrm{sig}}:=(\widetilde{u}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{u}_{r_{+}},\widetilde{u}_{n-r_{-}+1},\ldots,\widetilde{u}_{n}).

(2.9)

Thus
\widetilde{U}_{\mathrm{sig}}
collects the empirical right-edge and left-edge outliers corresponding to the positive and negative population spikes.
In this case, the relevant spectral gap is the distance from the signal spectrum to the bulk at
0
, namely

\lambda_{\min}:=\min_{1\leq j\leq r}|\lambda_{j}|.

The principal angle bound is given directly by the classical Davis-Kahan/Wedin theorem, so we only record the
2\to\infty
perturbation estimate for the full signal eigenspace. Perturbation bounds for
U,\widetilde{U}_{\rm sig}
follow by combining the positive- and negative-spike versions above.

C
OROLLARY
1

(Full signal eigenspace)
.

Fix
D>0
. Under Assumption
1.1
and one of Assumptions
1.2
and
1.3
, assume

\lambda_{\min}\geq 9\sqrt{R_{\max}}+165\sqrt{r}\rho_{k,D}+20\sqrt{r}\frac{\mathrm{osc}(\mathcal{R})}{\lambda_{\min}},

then, with probability at least
1-n^{-D}
,

\min_{O\in\mathbb{O}(r)}\|\widetilde{U}_{\mathrm{sig}}-UO\|_{2,\infty}\leq 42\sqrt{r}\|U\|_{2,\infty}\frac{\mathrm{osc}(\mathcal{R})}{\lambda_{\min}^{2}}+240\|U\|_{2,\infty}\sqrt{r}\frac{R_{\max}}{\lambda_{\min}^{2}}+480\sqrt{r}\frac{M_{D}}{\lambda_{\min}}.

R
EMARK
2.2

(Extension to rectangular matrices)
.

Our eigenspace perturbation results extend to singular subspace perturbation for rectangular matrices via the standard Hermitian dilation technique. See Appendix
G
for a brief discussion.

R
EMARK
2.3

(Other perturbation metrics)
.

Theorem
3
focuses on the operator-norm principal angle and the
2\to\infty
norm bounds for clarity. The proof framework is more general: it controls the block components of the empirical eigenspace along non-target signal directions and the null space of
A
. These estimates, combined with the techniques from
[
83
]
, extend to all standard metrics (Frobenius norm, unitarily invariant norms, linear forms, max norm, and weighted rowwise norms).

We focus on these two bounds because they reveal the key mechanisms most clearly. The operator-norm bound captures the projection of
\widetilde{U}_{k}
outside
U_{k}
, including components along lower positive spikes, negative spikes, and the null space. The
2\to\infty
bound translates this decomposition into rowwise estimates, showing how the variance-profile bias interacts with the incoherence of the signal eigenvector.

3
Geometric bias, sharpness, and debiasing

3.1
Interpretation of the geometric bias

The present variance-profile model introduces a new deterministic geometric bias term

\mathcal{B}_{k}=\frac{10\sqrt{k}}{\delta_{k}\lambda_{k}}\left(\|\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{J}\mathcal{I}}\|+8\frac{R_{\max}}{\lambda_{k}^{2}}\mathrm{osc}(\mathcal{R})\right)+4\sqrt{k}\frac{\|\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{N}\mathcal{K}}\|}{\lambda_{k}^{2}},

which captures the interaction between the signal eigenspaces and the inhomogeneous row-variance profile
\mathcal{R}=\mathrm{diag}(R_{i})
. In the homogeneous noise setting where
\mathcal{R}=cI
, the geometric bias term
\mathcal{B}_{k}
vanishes, our current bounds in Theorem
3
match the two-term bounds studied in
[
83
]
, up to logarithmic factors and rank factors.

The two quantities
\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{J}\mathcal{I}},\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{N}\mathcal{K}}
play different roles in the bias term. The term
\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{J}\mathcal{I}}
captures how the target top-
k
cluster interacts with the lower positive cluster
\{k+1,\dots,r_{+}\}
, appearing at the gap-dependent scale
(\delta_{k}\lambda_{k})^{-1}
. By contrast,
\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{N}\mathcal{K}}
captures interactions between negative and nonnegative signal directions, but at the weaker scale
\lambda_{k}^{-2}
. In practice, the contribution from
\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{N}\mathcal{K}}
is often negligible. For instance, when all spikes are nonnegative, we have
\mathcal{N}=\emptyset
and this term vanishes. Even when negative spikes exist, the term involving
\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{N}\mathcal{K}}
is usually dominated by
\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{J}\mathcal{I}}
as it has a stronger suppression factor
\lambda_{k}^{-2}
.

We believe that the leading geometric bias terms

\frac{\|\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{J}\mathcal{I}}\|}{\delta_{k}\lambda_{k}},\quad\frac{\|\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{N}\mathcal{K}}\|}{\lambda_{k}^{2}}

are intrinsic to the variance-profile model.

A rank-two model illustration.

To explain where these terms come from, consider a simple rank-2 model
A=\lambda_{1}u_{1}u_{1}^{\mathrm{T}}+\lambda_{2}u_{2}u_{2}^{\mathrm{T}}
with
\lambda_{1}>\lambda_{2}>0
and the leading empirical eigenvector
\widetilde{u}_{1}
. Let
Q
be the orthogonal projection onto the null space of
A
. From the decomposition

\widetilde{u}_{1}=u_{1}u_{1}^{\mathrm{T}}\widetilde{u}_{1}+u_{2}u_{2}^{\mathrm{T}}\widetilde{u}_{1}+Q\widetilde{u}_{1},

taking the
\ell_{2}
norm on both sides and rearranging terms, we have

\sin^{2}\angle(u_{1},\widetilde{u}_{1})=(u_{2}^{\mathrm{T}}\widetilde{u}_{1})^{2}+\|Q\widetilde{u}_{1}\|^{2}.

The term
u_{2}^{\mathrm{T}}\widetilde{u}_{1}
captures the interaction between signal directions. In our proofs, this quantity is controlled by projecting the eigenvector equation

\widetilde{u}_{1}=G(\widetilde{\lambda}_{1})A\widetilde{u}_{1}

onto
u_{2}
. To precisely estimate this term, from the eigenvector equation
(A+E)\widetilde{u}_{1}=\widetilde{\lambda}_{1}\widetilde{u}_{1}
, we solve for

\widetilde{u}_{1}=(\widetilde{\lambda}_{1}-E)^{-1}A\widetilde{u}_{1}=G(\widetilde{\lambda}_{1})A\widetilde{u}_{1}.

A key technical input in our proof is to show that the random resolvent
G
can be precisely approximated by its QVE approximation, a deterministic matrix
\Phi
(defined (
5.1
)). This
\Phi
isolates the deterministic contribution of the variance profile. Indeed, from

u_{2}^{\mathrm{T}}\widetilde{u}_{1}\approx u_{2}^{\mathrm{T}}\Phi(\widetilde{\lambda}_{1})A\widetilde{u}_{1}=\lambda_{1}(u_{2}^{\mathrm{T}}\Phi(\widetilde{\lambda}_{1})u_{1})u_{1}^{\mathrm{T}}\widetilde{u}_{1}+\lambda_{2}(u_{2}^{\mathrm{T}}\Phi(\widetilde{\lambda}_{1})u_{2})u_{2}^{\mathrm{T}}\widetilde{u}_{1},

plugging in the expansion of
\Phi\approx z^{-1}I+z^{-3}\mathcal{R}
in (
5.4
), the leading off-diagonal term is

u_{2}^{\mathrm{T}}\Phi(\widetilde{\lambda}_{1})u_{1}\approx\frac{u_{2}^{\mathrm{T}}\mathcal{R}u_{1}}{{\widetilde{\lambda}_{1}}^{3}}.

Substituting this back and approximating
\widetilde{\lambda}_{1}\approx\lambda_{1}
and
u_{1}^{\mathrm{T}}\widetilde{u}_{1}\approx 1
, we obtain

u_{2}^{\mathrm{T}}\widetilde{u}_{1}\approx\frac{1}{\lambda_{1}^{2}}(u_{2}^{\mathrm{T}}\mathcal{R}u_{1})+\frac{\lambda_{2}}{\lambda_{1}}u_{2}^{\mathrm{T}}\widetilde{u}_{1}.

Rearranging this equation to solve
u_{2}^{\mathrm{T}}\widetilde{u}_{1}
yields

|u_{2}^{\mathrm{T}}\widetilde{u}_{1}|\approx\frac{|u_{2}^{\mathrm{T}}\mathcal{R}u_{1}|}{\delta_{1}\lambda_{1}},

which mirrors the
\frac{\|\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{J}\mathcal{I}}\|}{\delta_{k}\lambda_{k}}
term in our theorem. If instead
\lambda_{1}>0>\lambda_{2}
, the same calculation reveals

|u_{2}^{\mathrm{T}}\widetilde{u}_{1}|\approx\frac{|u_{2}^{\mathrm{T}}\mathcal{R}u_{1}|}{\lambda_{1}(\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2})}=\frac{|u_{2}^{\mathrm{T}}\mathcal{R}u_{1}|}{\lambda_{1}(\lambda_{1}+|\lambda_{2}|)}.

We bound
\frac{|u_{2}^{\mathrm{T}}\mathcal{R}u_{1}|}{\lambda_{1}(\lambda_{1}+|\lambda_{2}|)}\leq\frac{|u_{2}^{\mathrm{T}}\mathcal{R}u_{1}|}{\lambda_{1}^{2}}
in our perturbation results for simplicity.

The preceding calculation explains the leading bias terms
\frac{\|\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{J}\mathcal{I}}\|}{\delta_{k}\lambda_{k}},\frac{\|\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{N}\mathcal{K}}\|}{\lambda_{k}^{2}}
. By contrast, the higher-order term in
\mathcal{B}_{k}
involving

\frac{R_{\max}}{\delta_{k}\lambda_{k}^{3}}\mathrm{osc}(\mathcal{R})

comes from bounding the diagonal remainder
\varepsilon(z)
in the second-order expansion of
\Phi(z)
in (
5.1
):

\Phi(z)=\frac{1}{z}I_{n}+\frac{1}{z^{3}}\mathcal{R}+\varepsilon(z).

We control this remainder using a uniform oscillation estimate, which may not be sharp. A higher-order expansion of
\Phi(z)
would reveal additional structured variance-profile terms beyond
\mathcal{R}
and replace the uniform
\mathrm{osc}(\mathcal{R})
bound with finer geometric couplings, yielding a sharper higher-order term. However, isolating these higher-order structures requires substantially more delicate analysis to control the deterministic remainders and extract the finer stochastic fluctuations. This is beyond the scope of the current paper.

3.2
Sharpness and numerical experiments

Before presenting numerical experiments, we briefly discuss the sharpness of our bounds. Our bounds contain three terms, compared to two terms in the homogeneous noise setting of
[
83
]
. In
[
83
]
for i.i.d. Gaussian noise, eigenspace perturbation is governed by two terms: a stochastic fluctuation term (
\propto\sqrt{r}/\delta_{k}
) and a signal-to-noise term (
\propto\|E\|/\lambda_{k}
). As shown in
[
83
, Section 4.1]
, these terms are near-optimal.

Under heterogeneous variance profiles, in Theorem
3
, a third term emerges: the geometric bias term
\mathcal{B}_{k}
. While the first two terms remain essentially unchanged (up to logarithmic factors and a
\sqrt{r}
factor), this new term captures the deterministic bias induced by variance heterogeneity. Since the first two terms were already validated in
[
83
]
, our simulations focus on the third term. We design experiments to isolate
\mathcal{B}_{k}
and confirm that it is not an artifact of loose bounds, but a visible deterministic effect in heterogeneous regimes.

Numerical simulations.

We consider a rank-2 model
A=\lambda_{1}u_{1}u_{1}^{\mathrm{T}}+\lambda_{2}u_{2}u_{2}^{\mathrm{T}}
with spikes
\lambda_{1}>\lambda_{2}>0
and eigenvectors
u_{1}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(e_{1}+e_{2})
and
u_{2}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(e_{1}-e_{2})
. The noise variance profile has row sums
\mathcal{R}_{\Delta}=\text{diag}(\rho+\Delta,\rho-\Delta,\rho,\dots,\rho)
, where
\rho>0
is the baseline variance and
0\leq\Delta\leq 0.8\rho
is the heterogeneity parameter. This gives the geometric coupling
\mathcal{V}_{12}=u_{2}^{\mathrm{T}}\mathcal{R}_{\Delta}u_{1}=\Delta
.

To show that geometric bias is deterministic rather than random, we compute the Monte Carlo average of
u_{2}^{\mathrm{T}}\widetilde{u}_{1}
, where in each trial we align the empirical eigenvector so that
u_{1}^{\mathrm{T}}\widetilde{u}_{1}\geq 0
. Since the noise is zero-mean and isotropic, averaging removes random fluctuations while preserving the deterministic bias. Figure
1
compares these empirical averages with our theoretical prediction
\frac{\Delta}{\delta_{1}\lambda_{1}}
for two different spectral gaps
\delta_{1}=\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}
.

(a)
\delta_{1}=10
.

(b)
\delta_{1}=20
.

Figure 1:
Geometric bias under varying spectral gaps. Setup: dimension
n=1200
, baseline noise
\rho=400
, top spike
\lambda_{1}=360
, heterogeneity parameter
\Delta\in\{0,16,32,\ldots,320\}
, with 300 Monte Carlo samples. Each panel shows the empirical alignment
\mathbb{E}[u_{2}^{\mathrm{T}}\widetilde{u}_{1}]
versus
\Delta
, compared with the theoretical prediction
\Delta/(\lambda_{1}\delta_{1})
.

3.3
Oracle de-biasing

The goal of this subsection is modest. We show, at the oracle level, that the leading
\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{J}\mathcal{I}}
-bias in
\mathcal{B}_{k}
can be removed. As discussed earlier,
\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{J}\mathcal{I}}
causes the main deviation from previous homogeneous bounds. When
k=r_{+}
,
\mathcal{J}=\emptyset
and this bias vanishes. For intermediate eigenspaces (
k<r_{+}
), correction may help when the deterministic bias dominates the stochastic fluctuations, i.e.,

\frac{\|\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{J}\mathcal{I}}\|}{\lambda_{k}}\gg M.

For
s\in[k]
, consider the block matrices
U_{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathrm{T}}\Phi(\widetilde{\lambda}_{s})U_{\mathcal{I}}
and
U_{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathrm{T}}\Phi(\widetilde{\lambda}_{s})U_{\mathcal{J}}
. Define

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}_{s}:=(I-U_{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathrm{T}}\Phi(\widetilde{\lambda}_{s})U_{\mathcal{J}}\Lambda_{\mathcal{J}})^{-1}U_{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathrm{T}}\Phi(\widetilde{\lambda}_{s})U_{\mathcal{I}}\Lambda_{\mathcal{I}}.

(3.1)

The next result guarantees that
\mathcal{T}_{s}
is well-defined on the event considered in Theorem
3
. Its proof is provided in Appendix
H.1
.

L
EMMA
3.1
.

Assume
\mathcal{J}=\{k+1,\ldots,r_{+}\}\neq\emptyset
. Under the assumptions and with the same probability as in Theorem
3
, for every
s\in[k]
,

\left\|\left(I-U_{\mathcal{J}}^{\mathrm{T}}\Phi(\widetilde{\lambda}_{s})U_{\mathcal{J}}\Lambda_{\mathcal{J}}\right)^{-1}\right\|\leq 60\frac{\widetilde{\lambda}_{s}}{\delta_{k}}.

Define the oracle corrected vectors

\displaystyle w_{s}^{\mathrm{orc}}:=P_{U}\widetilde{u}_{s}-U_{\mathcal{J}}{\mathcal{T}}_{s}U_{\mathcal{I}}^{\mathrm{T}}\widetilde{u}_{s},\qquad s=1,\ldots,k.

Let

W_{k}^{\mathrm{orc}}:=(w_{1}^{\mathrm{orc}},\ldots,w_{k}^{\mathrm{orc}}),\qquad U_{k}^{\mathrm{orc}}:=\orth(W_{k}^{\mathrm{orc}}).

(3.2)

We denote by
\orth(W)
the orthonormalization of the matrix
W
, which consists of orthonormal columns spanning the same subspace as
W
.

Define the oracle error term

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{k}^{\mathrm{orc}}:=4\sqrt{k}\frac{\|\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{N}\mathcal{K}}\|}{\lambda_{k}^{2}}+24\sqrt{k}\frac{R_{\max}}{{\lambda}_{k}^{4}}\mathrm{osc}(\mathcal{R})+124\sqrt{k}\frac{M_{D}}{\delta_{k}}.

P
ROPOSITION
3.1

(Oracle blockwise de-biasing)
.

Assume that the conditions of Theorem
3
hold. The following holds with the same probability as Theorem
3
:

\displaystyle\|\sin\angle(U_{k}^{\mathrm{orc}},U_{k})\|

\displaystyle\leq 2\mathcal{E}_{k}^{\mathrm{orc}},

(3.3)

\displaystyle\min_{O\in\mathbb{O}(k)}\|U_{k}^{\mathrm{orc}}-U_{k}O\|_{2,\infty}

\displaystyle\leq 3\|U\|_{2,\infty}\mathcal{E}_{k}^{\mathrm{orc}}.

(3.4)

The proof of Proposition
3.1
is deferred to Appendix
H.2
. The key point is that the oracle correction subtracts the deterministic contribution from the
\mathcal{J}
-block predicted by the QVE. This removes the leading
\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{J}\mathcal{I}}
-term in
\mathcal{B}_{k}
. Additionally, the projection
P_{U}
removes the null-space component, which explains why the signal-noise term

\|E\|/\lambda_{k}
in
Theorem
3
is absent from Proposition
3.1
.

The oracle estimator uses the unknown population eigenspace. A natural plug-in version replaces the population blocks by empirical outlier blocks. We provide a plug-in de-biasing implementation and explain its limitations in the Appendix
I
. Developing optimal data-driven bias correction methods is left for future work.

4
Related literature

This section highlights perturbation results relevant to heterogeneous random
noise. A broader review of matrix perturbation, rowwise eigenspace analysis,
and statistical applications can be found in
[
83
]
. Here we focus on
work most closely related to variance heterogeneity and random matrix
perturbation with non-identically distributed noise.

Perturbation bounds beyond worst-case theory.

Classical perturbation results, including Weyl’s inequality
[
86
]

and the Davis-Kahan-Wedin sin
\Theta
theorem
[
47
,
85
]
, control
eigenvalue and eigenspace errors through the operator norm of the perturbation
and the relevant spectral separation. While sharp for arbitrary deterministic perturbations, these bounds can be conservative for structured random perturbations. Modern refinements include population-gap
variants, Schatten and unitarily invariant norm bounds, perturbation
projection error bounds, and other deterministic or stochastic improvements;
see, for example,

[
91
,
82
,
34
,
67
,
93
,
84
,
7
,
19
,
95
,
80
,
72
,
24
]
.

Extensive work has also addressed entrywise and rowwise perturbation bounds. Representative works include

[
51
,
2
,
1
,
32
,
37
,
42
,
64
,
96
,
24
,
89
,
90
,
88
]
.
Perturbation of linear and bilinear forms of eigenvectors and singular vectors
has been studied in

[
63
,
87
,
49
,
65
,
44
,
3
,
4
]
. These results play an
important role in high-dimensional statistics, including matrix completion,
spectral clustering, ranking, community detection, submatrix localization, and
Gaussian mixture models; see, for example,

[
36
,
35
,
61
,
8
,
9
,
33
,
43
,
69
,
79
,
81
,
66
,
13
,
62
,
30
,
31
,
68
,
48
,
52
,
56
,
70
,
29
,
46
]
. For a statistical survey of spectral methods and perturbation
bounds, see
[
41
]
.

In the homogeneous Gaussian-noise setting,

[
73
,
83
]
obtained sharp singular-subspace perturbation estimates
showing that the error is governed by a signal-to-noise term and a stochastic
signal-space fluctuation term. Recent work of Tran and Vu

[
76
,
77
]
develops combinatorial contour-expansion and
relative-norm perturbation bounds that exploit structural interaction between
the signal and the perturbation. Our work shares the goal of moving beyond worst-case perturbation theory, but through a different mechanism:
we use variance-profile local laws and QVE expansions to identify a
deterministic geometric bias induced by heterogeneous noise.

Heterogeneous noise in statistical spectral problems.

There is a substantial statistical literature on spectral estimation under
heteroskedastic or heterogeneous noise. Zhang, Cai and Wu
[
92
]

introduced HeteroPCA, based on diagonal deletion and imputation, to correct
the bias caused by heterogeneous diagonal noise in spiked covariance models.
Yan, Chen and Fan
[
89
]
developed inference for heteroskedastic PCA with
missing data, obtaining distributional guarantees for the estimated principal
subspace and entrywise inference for the spiked covariance matrix. Related
work on high-dimensional heteroskedastic PCA and weighted PCA includes

[
58
,
59
,
60
]
. Zhang and Mondelli
[
94
]
recently
studied rank-one matrix denoising with doubly heteroscedastic noise and
derived asymptotic fundamental limits and optimal spectral methods.

These works are close in motivation but differ from ours in both model and
mechanism. Much of this literature concerns sample covariance, missing-data,
or rectangular denoising models, where the main issue is often diagonal-noise
bias correction, whitening, optimal shrinkage, or statistical inference. By
contrast, we study a symmetric additive signal-plus-noise model with
arbitrary entrywise variance profile and possible sparsity. The bias identified
here is not merely a diagonal variance bias; it is a geometric bias governed by

\mathcal{V}=U^{\mathrm{T}}\mathcal{R}U
, which records how the row-wise variance
profile changes the geometry of the signal eigenspaces.

We also cite related work on rowwise, entrywise, and inferential perturbation
for completeness, including

[
51
,
37
,
2
,
1
,
32
,
64
,
24
,
89
,
90
,
4
,
3
,
44
,
40
]
; these works concern fine-grained perturbation and
distributional inference in related spectral models, but they do not address
the variance-profile geometric bias studied here.

Related literature in random matrix theory.

Our work is also connected to the random matrix literature on finite-rank
deformations and outlier eigenvectors. The BBP transition was introduced by
Baik, Ben Arous and Péché
[
11
]
, with subsequent developments for
spiked covariance models, matrix denoising, and finite-rank deformations in

[
12
,
75
,
10
,
21
,
22
,
20
,
27
,
38
,
39
,
16
,
17
,
18
,
23
,
50
,
71
]
. The variance-profile setting is closely related to the
theory of general Wigner-type matrices and the quadratic vector equation.
Ajanki, Erdős and Krüger
[
6
]
developed the
QVE/Dyson-equation framework and proved local laws and universality for
matrices with general variance profiles. Our use of the QVE is more elementary
and takes place in the large-
z
outlier domain, where the solution is stable
and admits a direct expansion.

Recent asymptotic work has begun to study outliers, detection, and inference
limits in spiked models with variance-profile, inhomogeneous, or structured
noise. Bhattacharya, Chakrabarty and Hazra
[
26
]
analyze outlier
eigenvalues and eigenvectors of generalized Wigner matrices with finite-rank
deterministic perturbations. Guionnet, Ko, Krzakala and Zdeborová
[
55
]

study low-rank matrix estimation with inhomogeneous output channels and
detection thresholds. Bao, Cheong, Lee and Li
[
15
]
study signal
detection from spiked noise via asymmetrization. We also mention the physics
work of Ferreira and Metz
[
53
]
on the BBP transition in an inhomogeneous
rank-one spiked Wigner model. These results are complementary to ours: they
are mainly asymptotic and focus on limiting distributions, information limits,
detection thresholds, or BBP transitions, whereas our results are finite-sample
perturbation bounds for deterministic low-rank signals under sparse
independent variance-profile noise.

5
Deterministic QVE estimates and preliminary bounds

Consider the resolvent (Green function) of
E
:

G(z)=(zI-E)^{-1},\qquad z\in\mathbb{C}^{+}.

For
z\in\mathbb{C}^{+}
, define a deterministic diagonal matrix

\Phi\equiv\Phi(z)=\mathrm{diag}(\phi_{1}(z),\ldots,\phi_{n}(z))

where
\phi(z)=(\phi_{1}(z),\ldots,\phi_{n}(z))
is the solution to the Quadratic Vector Equations (QVE) given by:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\phi_{i}(z)}=z-\sum_{j=1}^{n}\sigma_{ij}^{2}\phi_{j}(z),\qquad i\in[n],

(5.1)

with
\operatorname{Im}\phi_{i}(z)<0
for
z\in\mathbb{C}^{+}
. Each
\phi_{i}(z)
is analytic
on
\mathbb{C}^{+}
. It is shown in
[
6
, Theorem 2.1]
that such a solution exists, is unique, and extends continuously to the real axis outside the spectrum (so in particular
\phi_{i}(z)>0
for
z
sufficiently large).

We collect deterministic estimates for the QVE solution
\Phi(z)
and elementary bounds for resolvents
G(z)
. These estimates are used in two places: to identify the deterministic locations of outlier eigenvalues, and to extract the geometric bias from the expansion of
\Phi(z)
.

Recall that

R_{i}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\sigma_{ij}^{2},\qquad R_{\max}=\max_{i}R_{i},\qquad\mathcal{R}=\operatorname{diag}(R_{1},\ldots,R_{n}).

The next result provides basic bounds on
\phi_{i}(z)
and the second-order expansion of these
\phi_{i}
’s for large
z
. The proof is provided in Appendix
A.1
.

L
EMMA
5.1
.

Assume
z\in\mathbb{C}
satisfying
|z|\geq\sqrt{6R_{\max}}
.

(i)
For every
i\in[n]
, we have

\frac{1}{2|z|}\leq|\phi_{i}(z)|\leq\frac{3}{2|z|}

and

\frac{3}{4}|z|\leq\big|z-\sum_{j}\sigma_{ij}^{2}\phi_{j}(z)\big|\leq\frac{5}{4}|z|.

Furthermore, we have

\displaystyle\|\Phi^{\prime}(z)\|=\max_{i}|\phi_{i}^{\prime}(z)|\leq\frac{4}{|z|^{2}}\quad\text{and}\quad\|\Phi^{\prime\prime}(z)\|=\max_{i}|\phi_{i}^{\prime\prime}(z)|\leq\frac{28}{|z|^{3}}.

(5.2)

(ii)
For
i\in[n]
, we have the second-order expansion:

\displaystyle\phi_{i}(z)=\frac{1}{z}+\frac{R_{i}}{z^{3}}+\varepsilon_{i}(z)\quad\text{with}\quad|\varepsilon_{i}(z)|\leq\frac{45}{8}\frac{R_{\max}^{2}}{|z|^{5}}.

(5.3)

In particular,

\displaystyle\Phi(z)=\frac{1}{z}I_{n}+\frac{1}{z^{3}}\mathcal{R}+\varepsilon(z),

(5.4)

where

\varepsilon(z)=\mathrm{diag}(\varepsilon_{1}(z),\cdots,\varepsilon_{n}(z))\quad\text{and}\quad\|\varepsilon(z)\|\leq\frac{45}{8}\frac{R_{\max}^{2}}{|z|^{5}}.

Furthermore, we have

\displaystyle\Phi^{\prime}(z)=-\frac{1}{z^{2}}I_{n}-\frac{3}{z^{4}}\mathcal{R}+\varepsilon^{\prime}(z),

(5.5)

where

\varepsilon^{\prime}(z)=\mathrm{diag}(\varepsilon_{1}^{\prime}(z),\cdots,\varepsilon_{n}^{\prime}(z))\quad\text{and}\quad\|\varepsilon^{\prime}(z)\|\leq\frac{243}{5}\frac{R_{\max}^{2}}{|z|^{6}}.

The entrywise bound in Lemma
5.1
is not sufficient for the
eigenspace perturbation theorem. For the error term
\varepsilon(z)=\mathrm{diag}(\varepsilon_{1}(z),\dots,\varepsilon_{n}(z))
in the second-order expansion of
\Phi(z)
in (
5.3
), we control its
oscillation
. For
z\in\mathbb{C}
satisfying
|z|\geq\sqrt{6R_{\max}}
, denote

\mathrm{osc}(\varepsilon(z)):=\max_{i,j}|\varepsilon_{i}(z)-\varepsilon_{j}(z)|.

(5.6)

If
z
is a real number, then
\mathrm{osc}(\varepsilon(z))=\max_{i}\varepsilon_{i}(z)-\min_{i}\varepsilon_{i}(z).
Recall that

\mathrm{osc}(\mathcal{R})=R_{\max}-R_{\min}.

The bound on
\mathrm{osc}(\varepsilon(z))
is given below; its proof is deferred to Appendix
A.2
.

L
EMMA
5.2
.

For any complex number
z
with
|z|\geq\sqrt{6R_{\max}}
, we have

\displaystyle\mathrm{osc}(\varepsilon(z))\leq\frac{15R_{\max}}{|z|^{5}}\mathrm{osc}(\mathcal{R}).

(5.7)

The same QVE expansion also determines the typical locations around
which the outlier eigenvalues concentrate. We prove the next result in Appendix
A.3
.

P
ROPOSITION
5.1

(Deterministic outlier location)
.

Let
j\leq r_{+}
and assume
\lambda_{j}\geq 6\sqrt{R_{\max}}
. Define

\widehat{\alpha}_{j}(z):=1-\lambda_{j}u_{j}^{\mathrm{T}}\Phi(z)u_{j}\quad\text{for}\quad z\geq 3\sqrt{R_{\max}}.

Then
\widehat{\alpha}_{j}
has a unique real zero
\vartheta_{j}\in[3\sqrt{R_{\max}},\infty)
.
Moreover, this zero is simple and satisfies

\displaystyle\left|\vartheta_{j}-\lambda_{j}-\frac{\mathcal{V}_{jj}}{\lambda_{j}}\right|\leq 145\frac{R_{\max}}{\lambda_{j}^{3}}\mathrm{osc}(\mathcal{R}).

(5.8)

Since
\widehat{\alpha}_{j}(\lambda_{j})
is increasing and
\widehat{\alpha}_{j}(\lambda_{j})\leq 0
, we also have

\displaystyle\vartheta_{j}\geq\lambda_{j}.

(5.9)

The following result provides crude bounds on the operator norms of Green functions
G(z)=(zI-E)^{-1}
and
G^{(k)}=(zI-E^{(k)})^{-1}
, where
E^{(k)}
is obtained from
E
with the
k
-th row and column replaced by zero.

L
EMMA
5.3

(Lemma 16 from
[
73
]
)
.

For
z\in\mathbb{C}
with
|z|\geq 2\|E\|
, we have

\displaystyle\|G(z)\|\leq\frac{2}{|z|},\quad\|G^{(k)}\|\leq\frac{2}{|z|}.

(5.10)

L
EMMA
5.4

(Bound on the noise matrix)
.

Let
E=(E_{ij})_{n\times n}
be a symmetric matrix whose entries are centered and independent (up to symmetry) random variables. Assume the entries
E_{ij}
’s are sub-Gaussian with
\|E_{ij}\|_{\psi_{2}}\leq K\sigma_{ij}.
Denote
R_{\max}=\max_{i}\sum_{j}\sigma_{ij}^{2}
.
Then for any
D>0
, we have

\mathbb{P}\left(\|E\|\leq 2.9\sqrt{R_{\max}}+cK\sigma_{\max}{(D+2)\log n}\right)\geq 1-3n^{-D}

for an absolute constant
c>0
.

The result is a direct consequence of the sharp non-asymptotic norm bounds established in Bandeira and van Handel
[
14
]
. We provide a short derivation in Appendix
A.4
.

6
Proof strategy and technical inputs

This section states the probabilistic inputs used in the proof of
Theorem
3
and explains how they combine with the
deterministic QVE estimates from Section
5
. The two
main probabilistic ingredients are the isotropic local laws for the
resolvent
G(z)
and the location theorem for the outlier eigenvalues of

A+E
.

6.1
Isotropic local laws

Consider
G(z)=(zI-E)^{-1}
for
z\in\mathbb{C}^{+}
. Recall the QVE approximation
\Phi(z)
defined in (
5.1
). Our first main technical step is to establish an isotropic local law for
G(z)
. Denote

\displaystyle\mathfrak{m}_{D}:=\begin{cases}C_{\rm gen}^{\prime}(D+6)^{3/2}\beta K\sigma_{\max}(\log n)^{2},&\text{under Assumption~\ref{assump:extra}},\\[2.84526pt]
C_{\rm bd}^{\prime}(D+6)(c_{0}+\beta_{0})K\sigma_{\max}\log n,&\text{under Assumption~\ref{assump:extra-weaker}}.\end{cases}

(6.1)

Here,
C_{\rm gen}^{\prime}
and
C_{\rm bd}^{\prime}
are sufficiently large absolute constants.

T
HEOREM
4

(Isotropic local law)
.

Fix
D>0
. Suppose Assumption
1.1
and one of
Assumptions
1.2
and
1.3
hold. For any deterministic unit vectors
v,w\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
and every fixed
z\in\mathbb{C}
with
|z|\geq 6\sqrt{R_{\max}}
, we have

\left|v^{\mathrm{T}}(G(z)-\Phi(z))w\right|\leq\frac{\mathfrak{m}_{D}}{|z|^{2}}

with probability at least
1-n^{-D-4}
.

Theorem
4
is proved in Section
8
. We also need a version that is uniform over the spectral domain where the
outlier eigenvalues are located. Define

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}_{\rm out}:=\left\{z\in\mathbb{C}:6\sqrt{R_{\max}}\leq|z|\leq 2R_{\max}^{3}\right\}.

(6.2)

This result is a direct consequence of Theorem
4
; its proof is provided in Appendix
E
. Recall that
A=U\Lambda U^{\mathrm{T}}
.

C
OROLLARY
2

(Uniform compressed local law)
.

Fix
D>0
. Suppose the assumptions of Theorem
4
hold. Then, with probability at least
1-n^{-D}
,

\displaystyle\sup_{z\in\mathcal{S}_{\rm out}}|z|^{2}\|U^{\mathrm{T}}(G(z)-\Phi(z))U\|\leq C^{\prime}r\cdot{\mathfrak{m}_{D}}=M_{D},

(6.3)

\displaystyle\sup_{z\in\mathcal{S}_{\rm out}}|z|^{2}\max_{1\leq i\leq n}\|e_{i}^{\mathrm{T}}(G(z)-\Phi(z))U\|\leq M_{D}.

(6.4)

Consequently,

\sup_{z\in\mathcal{S}_{\rm out}}|z|^{2}\max_{1\leq i\leq n}\left\|e_{i}^{\mathrm{T}}Q(G(z)-\Phi(z))U\right\|\leq 2M_{D},

(6.5)

where
Q=I-UU^{\mathrm{T}}
and
M_{D}
is defined in (
2.1
).

6.2
Outlier eigenvalue location

We next state the outlier-location results used in the proof of the eigenspace perturbation results. For any
\lambda_{j}\geq 6\sqrt{R_{\max}}
, Proposition
5.1
guarantees that there exists a unique solution
\vartheta_{j}
to the equation
\lambda_{j}^{-1}-u_{j}^{\mathrm{T}}\Phi(z)u_{j}=0
for
z\geq 3\sqrt{R_{\max}}
. Besides,

\vartheta_{j}\approx\lambda_{j}+\frac{\mathcal{V}_{jj}}{\lambda_{j}}.

For a simple supercritical eigenvalue
\lambda_{k}
, we will show that
\vartheta_{k}
is the typical location of
\widetilde{\lambda}_{k}
.

Recall that

\rho_{k}\equiv\rho_{k,D}:=10M_{D}+15\frac{\mathrm{osc}(\mathcal{R})}{\lambda_{k}}.

Denote the index set for the supercritical eigenvalues as

\displaystyle\mathcal{O}_{+}:=\{l\in[r_{+}]:\lambda_{l}\geq 6\sqrt{R_{\max}}\}.

(6.6)

T
HEOREM
5

(Individual outlier eigenvalue location)
.

Fix
D>0
and
k\in[r_{+}]
. If
\lambda_{k}\geq 6\sqrt{R_{\max}}+100\rho_{k}
is a simple eigenvalue and the gap condition holds:

\displaystyle\min_{l<k}(\vartheta_{l}-\vartheta_{k})

\displaystyle\geq 50\rho_{k}\quad\text{and}\quad\min_{l\in\mathcal{O}_{+},\,l>k}(\vartheta_{k}-\vartheta_{l})\geq 50\rho_{k}

with the convention that the minimum over an empty set is
+\infty
, then, with probability at least
1-n^{-D}
,

|\widetilde{\lambda}_{k}-\vartheta_{k}|\leq\rho_{k}.

The proof of Theorem
5
is given in Section
9
. Furthermore, we provide the following result on the outlier eigenvalue clusters, which will be used in the proof of the top-
k
eigenspace perturbations.

T
HEOREM
6

(Lower edge of the top-
k
outlier clusters)
.

Fix
D>0
and
k\in[r_{+}]
. Define

\displaystyle\underline{\vartheta}_{k}:=\min_{1\leq t\leq k}\vartheta_{t}.

(6.7)

If
\lambda_{k}\geq 6\sqrt{R_{\max}}+100\rho_{k}
and the gap condition holds:

\displaystyle\min_{l\in\mathcal{O}_{+},\,l>k}(\underline{\vartheta}_{k}-\vartheta_{l})\geq 50\rho_{k}

(6.8)

with the convention that the minimum over an empty set is
+\infty
, then, with probability at least
1-n^{-D}
,

\widetilde{\lambda}_{k}\geq\underline{\vartheta}_{k}-\rho_{k}.

In particular, for every
s\in[k]
,

\widetilde{\lambda}_{s}\geq\underline{\vartheta}_{k}-\rho_{k}.

R
EMARK
6.1
.

Theorem
6
holds under a stronger but more conventional assumption that the
k
-th deterministic root lower-bounds the preceding roots (i.e.,
\vartheta_{l}\geq\vartheta_{k}
for all
l<k
). Under that assumption,
\underline{\vartheta}_{k}=\vartheta_{k}
. However, we introduce
\underline{\vartheta}_{k}
to avoid the internal ordering of these
\vartheta_{s}
’s for
s\leq k
. Indeed, the deterministic roots can physically cross and permute, even when the underlying
\lambda_{s}
’s are strictly ordered. For instance, even if
\lambda_{l}>\lambda_{s}
for
l<s\in[k]
, as given in Proposition
5.1
,
\vartheta_{l}\approx\lambda_{l}+\frac{\mathcal{V}_{ll}}{\lambda_{l}}
could be dominated by
\vartheta_{s}\approx\lambda_{s}+\frac{\mathcal{V}_{ss}}{\lambda_{s}}
. This is because the shift
\mathcal{V}_{ll}=u_{l}^{\mathrm{T}}\mathcal{R}u_{l}
depends on the alignment of the
l
-th population eigenvector with the variance profile
\mathcal{R}
. In the presence of anisotropic noise, it is entirely possible for a lower-ranked spike’s variance projection (e.g.,
\mathcal{V}_{ss}
) to be significantly larger than that of a higher-ranked spike (e.g.,
\mathcal{V}_{ll}
).

The proof of Theorem
6
parallels that of Theorem
5
and can be found in Section
9
.

6.3
Proof roadmap

We briefly explain how the technical inputs above are used to prove
Theorem
3
. The proof is carried out on a
high-probability event where
\|E\|\leq 3\sqrt{R_{\max}}
and the isotropic local laws Theorem
4
and Corollary
2
hold.

The signal condition
\lambda_{k}\geq 9\sqrt{R_{\max}}

ensures that the outlier eigenvalues satisfy

\displaystyle\widetilde{\lambda}_{s}\geq\lambda_{k}-\|E\|\geq 6\sqrt{R_{\max}}

(6.9)

for all
s\in[k]
by Weyl’s inequalty. Thus the uniform local law Corollary
2
applies at the random eigenvalues
z=\widetilde{\lambda}_{s}
.

The deterministic part of the proof starts from the eigenvector equation

\widetilde{u}_{s}=G(\widetilde{\lambda}_{s})A\widetilde{u}_{s},\qquad s\in[k].

Projecting this identity onto the non-target signal directions and the null space gives separate bounds for how much
\widetilde{u}_{s}
extends outside the target space. The null-space projection produces the usual signal-to-noise contribution

\|E\|/\lambda_{k}
. For the signal-space terms, we decompose

G(z)=\Phi(z)+\Xi(z),\qquad\Xi(z):=G(z)-\Phi(z).

The compressed local law controls the contribution of
\Xi(z)
, while the
large-
z
expansion of
\Phi(z)
identifies the deterministic
variance-profile contribution. This yields the stochastic term involving

M_{D}
and the geometric bias term
\mathcal{B}_{k}
. The final operator-norm
and
2\to\infty
estimates follow from these blockwise bounds through deterministic reductions.

7
Proof of the main perturbation bounds: Theorem
3

Set

\displaystyle\Xi(z):=G(z)-\Phi(z).

(7.1)

Fix
D>0
. Define the events

\mathcal{E}_{\rm op}:=\{\|E\|\leq 3\sqrt{R_{\max}}\}

and

\mathcal{E}_{\rm loc}(D):=\left\{\sup_{z\in\mathcal{S}}|z|^{2}\|U^{\mathrm{T}}\Xi(z)U\|\leq M_{D}\right\}\cap\left\{\sup_{z\in\mathcal{S}_{\rm out}}|z|^{2}\max_{1\leq i\leq n}\left\|e_{i}^{\mathrm{T}}Q\Xi(z)U\right\|\leq 2M_{D}\right\},

where
\mathcal{S}_{\rm out}:=\left\{z\in\mathbb{C}:6\sqrt{R_{\max}}\leq|z|\leq 2R_{\max}^{3}\right\}
and
Q=I-UU^{\mathrm{T}}
.
Set

\mathcal{E}_{D}:=\mathcal{E}_{\rm op}\cap\mathcal{E}_{\rm loc}(D).

By Lemma
5.4
and Corollary
2
, we have

\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{E}_{D})\geq 1-n^{-D}.

All estimates below are deterministic on
\mathcal{E}_{D}
, except when the
outlier-location theorem is invoked. In that step we intersect with the
corresponding outlier-location event, which still has probability at least

1-n^{-D}
after adjusting constants.

On the event
\mathcal{E}_{D}
, note that our signal assumption
9\sqrt{R_{\max}}\leq\lambda_{k}\leq R_{\max}^{3}
ensures
\widetilde{\lambda}_{s}\in\mathcal{S}_{\rm out}
for all
s\leq k
by Weyl’s inequality (see (
6.9
)).

For simplicity, we write

M\equiv M_{D}.

We first present deterministic bounds on the difference between the eigenspaces
U_{k}
and
\widetilde{U}_{k}
. Let
J=[r]\setminus[k]
denote the complement index set and
U_{J}=(u_{k+1},\dots,u_{r})
. Let
P_{W}
be the orthogonal projection onto the subspace
W
.

P
ROPOSITION
7.1
.

For
1\leq k\leq r+
, assume
\lambda_{k}\geq 2\|E\|
. Then the following deterministic bounds hold.

(i)
For the operator norm bound, we have

\displaystyle\|\sin\angle(U_{k},\widetilde{U}_{k})\|\leq\|U_{J}^{\mathrm{T}}\widetilde{U}_{k}\|_{F}+2\frac{\|E\|}{\lambda_{k}}.

(7.2)

(ii)
For the
2\to\infty
norm bound, we have

\displaystyle\min_{O\in\mathbb{O}(k)}\|\widetilde{U}_{k}-U_{k}O\|_{2,\infty}\leq\|\widetilde{U}_{k}-P_{U_{k}}\widetilde{U}_{k}\|_{2,\infty}+\|U_{k}\|_{2,\infty}\|\sin\angle(U_{k},\widetilde{U}_{k})\|^{2},

(7.3)

where

\displaystyle\|\widetilde{U}_{k}-P_{U_{k}}\widetilde{U}_{k}\|_{2,\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\|U\|_{2,\infty}\cdot\|U_{J}^{\mathrm{T}}\widetilde{U}_{k}\|_{F}+\frac{13}{2}\sqrt{k}\|U\|_{2,\infty}\frac{R_{\max}}{\widetilde{\lambda}_{k}^{2}}

\displaystyle+4\sqrt{\sum_{s=1}^{k}\widetilde{\lambda}_{s}^{2}\max_{1\leq i\leq n}\left\|e_{i}^{\mathrm{T}}Q\Xi(z)U\right\|^{2}}.

(7.4)

Given these deterministic bounds, the main technical contribution of our perturbation analysis is the estimation of

\|U_{J}^{\mathrm{T}}\widetilde{U}_{k}\|_{F}=\sqrt{\sum_{s=1}^{k}\|U_{J}^{\mathrm{T}}\widetilde{u}_{s}\|^{2}}.

We first present a deterministic bound for each
\|U_{J}^{\mathrm{T}}\widetilde{u}_{s}\|
. The geometric bias matrix
\mathcal{V}=U^{\mathrm{T}}\mathcal{R}U
is defined in (
1.4
). For any index sets
\mathtt{I},\mathtt{J}\in[r]
with
U_{\mathtt{I}}=(u_{i})_{i\in\mathtt{I}}
and
U_{\mathtt{J}}=(u_{j})_{j\in\mathtt{J}}
, we denote

\mathcal{V}_{\mathtt{I}\mathtt{J}}:=U_{\mathtt{I}}^{\mathrm{T}}\mathcal{R}\,U_{\mathtt{J}}.

For a matrix
B
, we denote by
\mathrm{Off}(B)
the matrix obtained by setting all diagonal entries to zero.

P
ROPOSITION
7.2
.

Define index sets

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}:=\{k+1,\dots,r_{+}\},\quad\mathcal{I}:=[r]\setminus\mathcal{J},

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}:=\{r_{+}+1,\dots,r\},\quad\mathcal{K}:=[r]\setminus\mathcal{N}.

Fix
s\in[k]
. Define the gap

\Delta_{\mathcal{J}}^{\Phi}(\widetilde{\lambda}_{s}):=\widetilde{\lambda}_{s}\min_{j\in\mathcal{J}}|1-\lambda_{j}u_{j}^{\mathrm{T}}\Phi(\widetilde{\lambda}_{s})u_{j}|

with the convention that the minimum over an empty set is
+\infty
.
If the following gap condition holds:

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathcal{J}}^{\Phi}(\widetilde{\lambda}_{s})\geq\frac{2\lambda_{k+1}}{\widetilde{\lambda}_{s}^{2}}\|\mathrm{Off}(\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{J}\mathcal{J}})\|+\frac{13\lambda_{k+1}R_{\max}}{\widetilde{\lambda}_{s}^{4}}\mathrm{osc}(\mathcal{R}),

(7.5)

then we have

\displaystyle\|U_{J}^{\mathrm{T}}\widetilde{u}_{s}\|\leq

\displaystyle\frac{4}{\Delta_{\mathcal{J}}^{\Phi}(\widetilde{\lambda}_{s})}\left(\frac{\|\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{J}\mathcal{I}}\|}{\widetilde{\lambda}_{s}}+\frac{13}{2}\frac{R_{\max}}{\widetilde{\lambda}_{s}^{3}}\mathrm{osc}(\mathcal{R})+\widetilde{\lambda}_{s}^{2}\|U^{\mathrm{T}}\Xi(\widetilde{\lambda}_{s})U\|\right)

(7.6)

\displaystyle+2\sqrt{3}\left(\frac{\|\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{N}\mathcal{K}}\|}{\widetilde{\lambda}_{s}^{2}}+\frac{13}{2}\frac{R_{\max}}{\widetilde{\lambda}_{s}^{4}}\mathrm{osc}(\mathcal{R})+\widetilde{\lambda}_{s}\|U^{\mathrm{T}}\Xi(\widetilde{\lambda}_{s})U\|\right).

(7.7)

Note that when
\mathcal{J}=\emptyset
, we have
\Delta_{\mathcal{J}}^{\Phi}(\widetilde{\lambda}_{s})=\infty
, so the corresponding term on the right-hand side of (
7.6
) vanishes. The proofs of Propositions
7.1
and
7.2
are deferred to Appendix
B
.
On the event
\mathcal{E}_{D}
, the condition
\lambda_{k}\geq 9\sqrt{R_{\max}}
implies
\lambda_{k}\geq 3\|E\|\geq 2\|E\|,
so Proposition
7.1
applies.

Now we continue with the estimation of
\|U_{J}^{\mathrm{T}}\widetilde{u}_{s}\|
and split the discussion into two cases:
k=r_{+}
or
k<r_{+}
.

Case 1.
Assume
k=r_{+}
. Then
\mathcal{J}=\emptyset
and
\Delta_{\mathcal{J}}^{\Phi}(\widetilde{\lambda}_{s})=+\infty
. In this case,

J=\mathcal{N}=\{r_{+}+1,\dots,r\}.

We just bound

\|U_{J}^{\mathrm{T}}\widetilde{u}_{s}\|\leq 2\sqrt{3}\left(\frac{\|\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{N}\mathcal{K}}\|}{\widetilde{\lambda}_{s}^{2}}+\frac{13}{2}\frac{R_{\max}}{\widetilde{\lambda}_{s}^{4}}\mathrm{osc}(\mathcal{R})+\widetilde{\lambda}_{s}\|U^{\mathrm{T}}\Xi(\widetilde{\lambda}_{s})U\|\right).

By Theorem
6
(note that the gap condition (
6.8
) holds trivially), for every
s\in[k]
,

\widetilde{\lambda}_{s}\geq\underline{\vartheta}_{k}-\rho_{k}\geq\lambda_{k}-\rho_{k}\geq\frac{99}{100}\lambda_{k}.

Thus,

\displaystyle\|U_{J}^{\mathrm{T}}\widetilde{u}_{s}\|=\|U_{\mathcal{N}}^{\mathrm{T}}\widetilde{u}_{s}\|<4\frac{\|\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{N}\mathcal{K}}\|}{\lambda_{k}^{2}}+24\frac{R_{\max}}{{\lambda}_{k}^{4}}\mathrm{osc}(\mathcal{R})+4\frac{M}{\lambda_{k}}

(7.8)

and

\displaystyle\|U_{J}^{\mathrm{T}}\widetilde{U}_{k}\|_{F}=\sqrt{\sum_{s=1}^{k}\|U_{J}^{\mathrm{T}}\widetilde{u}_{s}\|^{2}}\leq\sqrt{r_{+}}\left(4\frac{\|\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{N}\mathcal{K}}\|}{\lambda_{r_{+}}^{2}}+24\frac{R_{\max}}{{\lambda}_{r_{+}}^{4}}\mathrm{osc}(\mathcal{R})+4\frac{M}{\lambda_{r_{+}}}\right).

(7.9)

Case 2.
Assume
k<r_{+}
. Under our gap assumption

\displaystyle\delta_{k}\geq 65\rho_{k}+20\frac{\mathrm{osc}(\mathcal{R})}{\lambda_{k}}\geq 65\rho_{k}+20\frac{\lambda_{k+1}}{\lambda_{k}^{2}}\mathrm{osc}(\mathcal{R}),

(7.10)

we first show that (
6.8
) holds, that is,

\min_{l\in\mathcal{O}_{+},\,l>k}(\underline{\vartheta}_{k}-\vartheta_{l})\geq 50\rho_{k}\quad\text{where}\quad\underline{\vartheta}_{k}=\min_{1\leq t\leq k}\vartheta_{t}.

If
\mathcal{O}_{+}\setminus[k]=\emptyset
, then the inequality holds because the left-hand side is
+\infty
by our notation. Assume
\mathcal{O}_{+}\setminus[k]\neq\emptyset
.

Suppose

\underline{\vartheta}_{k}=\vartheta_{t}

for some
1\leq t\leq k
. Observe from (
5.9
) that

\displaystyle\vartheta_{t}\geq\lambda_{t}\geq\lambda_{k}>\lambda_{k+1}\geq\lambda_{j}

(7.11)

for any
j\in\mathcal{J}=\{k+1,\dots,r_{+}\}
.

Define two auxiliary functions

\mathcal{L}(x):=x+\frac{R_{\min}}{x}-145\frac{R_{\max}}{x^{3}}\mathrm{osc}(\mathcal{R}),\quad\mathcal{U}(x):=x+\frac{R_{\max}}{x}+145\frac{R_{\max}}{x^{3}}\mathrm{osc}(\mathcal{R})

for
x\geq 6\sqrt{R_{\max}}
. Note that both
\mathcal{L}(x)
and
\mathcal{U}(x)
are increasing functions. Note that
\lambda_{t}\geq 9\sqrt{R_{\max}}
. Thus,

\mathcal{L}(\lambda_{t})\leq\vartheta_{t}\leq\mathcal{U}(\lambda_{t}).

For any
l\in\mathcal{O}_{+}
satisfying
l>k
, since
\lambda_{l}\leq\lambda_{k+1}
, by the definition of
\vartheta_{l}
for and Proposition
5.1
, we have

\mathcal{L}(\lambda_{l})\leq\vartheta_{l}\leq\mathcal{U}(\lambda_{l})\leq\mathcal{U}(\lambda_{k+1}).

In particular, for any
l\in\mathcal{O}_{+}\setminus[k]
(note that
t\in\mathcal{O}_{+}
), we have

\displaystyle\vartheta_{t}-\vartheta_{l}

\displaystyle\geq\mathcal{L}(\lambda_{t})-\mathcal{U}(\lambda_{k+1})

\displaystyle=(\lambda_{t}-\lambda_{k+1})+\frac{R_{\min}}{\lambda_{t}}-\frac{R_{\max}}{\lambda_{k+1}}-145\frac{R_{\max}}{\lambda_{t}^{3}}\mathrm{osc}(\mathcal{R})-145\frac{R_{\max}}{\lambda_{k+1}^{3}}\mathrm{osc}(\mathcal{R}).

Rewriting

\frac{R_{\min}}{\lambda_{t}}-\frac{R_{\max}}{\lambda_{k+1}}=\frac{\lambda_{k+1}-\lambda_{t}}{\lambda_{t}\lambda_{k+1}}R_{\min}+\frac{R_{\min}-R_{\max}}{\lambda_{k+1}}

and using the assumption
\lambda_{t},\lambda_{k+1}\geq 9\sqrt{R_{\max}}\geq 6\sqrt{R_{\min}}
, we further get

\displaystyle\vartheta_{t}-\vartheta_{l}

\displaystyle\geq\left(1-\frac{R_{\min}}{\lambda_{t}\lambda_{k+1}}\right)(\lambda_{t}-\lambda_{k+1})+\frac{R_{\min}-R_{\max}}{\lambda_{k+1}}-\frac{145}{36}\frac{\mathrm{osc}(\mathcal{R})}{\lambda_{t}}-\frac{145}{36}\frac{\mathrm{osc}(\mathcal{R})}{\lambda_{k+1}}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{35}{36}\delta_{k}-\left(1+\frac{145}{36}\right)\frac{\mathrm{osc}(\mathcal{R})}{\lambda_{k+1}}-\frac{145}{36}\frac{\mathrm{osc}(\mathcal{R})}{\lambda_{t}}

\displaystyle\geq\frac{35}{36}\delta_{k}-\frac{181}{36}\frac{\mathrm{osc}(\mathcal{R})}{\lambda_{k+1}}-\frac{145}{36}\frac{\mathrm{osc}(\mathcal{R})}{\lambda_{k}},

where we used
\lambda_{t}\geq\lambda_{k}
in the last inequality. To proceed, note that

\frac{\mathrm{osc}(\mathcal{R})}{\lambda_{k+1}}=\frac{\mathrm{osc}(\mathcal{R})}{\lambda_{k}}+\delta_{k}\frac{\mathrm{osc}(\mathcal{R})}{\lambda_{k}\lambda_{k+1}}\leq\frac{\mathrm{osc}(\mathcal{R})}{\lambda_{k}}+\frac{1}{36}\delta_{k}.

We get

\displaystyle\vartheta_{t}-\vartheta_{l}

\displaystyle\geq\left(\frac{35}{36}-\frac{181}{36^{2}}\right)\delta_{k}-\frac{326}{36}\frac{\mathrm{osc}(\mathcal{R})}{\lambda_{k}}\geq 50\rho_{k}

by our assumption (
7.10
) on
\delta_{k}
. Hence, the conclusion of Theorem
6
holds. In particular, for every
s\in[k]
,

\displaystyle\widetilde{\lambda}_{s}\geq\underline{\vartheta}_{k}-\rho_{k}={\vartheta}_{t}-\rho_{k}.

(7.12)

Recall
\Delta_{\mathcal{J}}^{\Phi}
defined in Proposition
7.2
. Next, we show that for any
s\in[k]
,

\displaystyle\Delta_{\mathcal{J}}^{\Phi}(\widetilde{\lambda}_{s})=\widetilde{\lambda}_{s}\min_{j\in\mathcal{J}}|1-\lambda_{j}u_{j}^{\mathrm{T}}\Phi(\widetilde{\lambda}_{s})u_{j}|\geq\frac{1}{2}\delta_{k}.

(7.13)

Denote
\alpha_{j}(z)=\lambda_{j}^{-1}-u_{j}^{\mathrm{T}}\Phi(z)u_{j}
for each
j\in[r]
. Then

\Delta_{\mathcal{J}}^{\Phi}(\widetilde{\lambda}_{s})=\min_{j\in\mathcal{J}}\big|\widetilde{\lambda}_{s}\lambda_{j}\alpha_{j}(\widetilde{\lambda}_{s})\big|.

For each
j\in\mathcal{J}
, by the expansion of
\Phi(z)
in (
5.4
), we have

\displaystyle\alpha_{j}(z)=\frac{1}{\lambda_{j}}-u_{j}^{\mathrm{T}}\Phi(z)u_{j}=\frac{1}{\lambda_{j}}-\frac{1}{z}-\frac{\mathcal{V}_{jj}}{z^{3}}-\varepsilon_{jj}(z),

where
\varepsilon_{jj}(z):=u_{j}^{\mathrm{T}}\varepsilon(z)u_{j}
.
Similarly,

\displaystyle\alpha_{t}(z)=\frac{1}{\lambda_{t}}-\frac{1}{z}-\frac{\mathcal{V}_{tt}}{z^{3}}-\varepsilon_{tt}(z).

From the difference
\alpha_{j}(z)-\alpha_{t}(z)
, we find

\displaystyle\alpha_{j}(z)=\left(\frac{1}{\lambda_{j}}-\frac{1}{\lambda_{t}}\right)+\alpha_{t}(z)-\frac{\mathcal{V}_{jj}-\mathcal{V}_{tt}}{z^{3}}-(\varepsilon_{jj}(z)-\varepsilon_{tt}(z)).

Therefore,

\displaystyle\widetilde{\lambda}_{s}\lambda_{j}\alpha_{j}(\widetilde{\lambda}_{s})

\displaystyle=\widetilde{\lambda}_{s}\left(1-\frac{\lambda_{j}}{\lambda_{t}}\right)+\widetilde{\lambda}_{s}\lambda_{j}\alpha_{t}(\widetilde{\lambda}_{s})-\frac{\lambda_{j}}{\widetilde{\lambda}_{s}^{2}}\left(\mathcal{V}_{jj}-\mathcal{V}_{tt}\right)-\widetilde{\lambda}_{s}\lambda_{j}(\varepsilon_{jj}(\widetilde{\lambda}_{s})-\varepsilon_{tt}(\widetilde{\lambda}_{s}))

\displaystyle:=T_{1}+T_{2}+T_{3}+T_{4}.

It follows that

\displaystyle\left|\widetilde{\lambda}_{s}\lambda_{j}\alpha_{j}(\widetilde{\lambda}_{s})\right|\geq T_{1}+T_{2}-|T_{3}|-|T_{4}|.

We estimate
T_{i}
’s respectively. For
T_{1}
, by (
7.11
), we have

\displaystyle T_{1}=\widetilde{\lambda}_{s}\left(1-\frac{\lambda_{j}}{\lambda_{t}}\right)\geq\widetilde{\lambda}_{s}\left(1-\frac{\lambda_{k+1}}{\lambda_{k}}\right)=\frac{\widetilde{\lambda}_{s}}{\lambda_{k}}\delta_{k}.

Then, by (
7.12
), we get

\displaystyle T_{1}\geq(\vartheta_{t}-\rho_{k})\frac{\delta_{k}}{\lambda_{k}}=\frac{\vartheta_{t}}{\lambda_{k}}\delta_{k}-\rho_{k}\frac{\delta_{k}}{\lambda_{k}}\geq\delta_{k}-\rho_{k}

since
\frac{\vartheta_{t}}{\lambda_{k}}\geq 1
by (
7.11
) and
\frac{\delta_{k}}{\lambda_{k}}\leq 1
.

Next, we estimate
T_{2}=\widetilde{\lambda}_{s}\lambda_{j}\alpha_{t}(\widetilde{\lambda}_{s})
. By the same estimates as (
9.28
) and (
9.29
), we have

\frac{5}{2x^{2}}\leq\alpha_{t}^{\prime}(x)\leq\frac{2}{x^{2}}\quad\text{for }x\geq 3\sqrt{R_{\max}}.

In particular,
\alpha_{t}(x)
is increasing for
x\geq 3\sqrt{R_{\max}}
. By our definition,
\alpha_{t}(\vartheta_{t})
=0. Since
\widetilde{\lambda}_{s}\geq\vartheta_{t}-\rho_{k}
by (
7.12
),

\alpha_{t}(\widetilde{\lambda}_{s})\geq\alpha_{t}(\vartheta_{t}-\rho_{k})=\alpha_{t}(\vartheta_{t}-\rho_{k})-\alpha_{t}(\vartheta_{t})=-\rho_{k}\alpha^{\prime}_{t}(\zeta),

where the last equation is due to the mean value theorem and
\zeta
is a value between
\vartheta_{t}-\rho_{k}
and
\vartheta_{t}
.
Then, by (
7.11
),

\widetilde{\lambda}_{s}\lambda_{j}\alpha_{t}(\widetilde{\lambda}_{s})\geq-\widetilde{\lambda}_{s}\lambda_{j}\frac{2}{\zeta^{2}}\rho_{k}\geq-2\frac{\widetilde{\lambda}_{s}\lambda_{k+1}}{(\vartheta_{t}-\rho_{k})^{2}}\rho_{k}.

Since
\vartheta_{t}-\rho_{k}\geq\frac{99}{100}\vartheta_{t}
and
\widetilde{\lambda}_{s}\leq\frac{3}{2}\lambda_{s}\leq\frac{3}{2}\vartheta_{s}
by Weyl’s inequality and (
5.9
), we have

\displaystyle T_{2}\geq-2(\frac{100}{99})^{2}\frac{\frac{3}{2}\vartheta_{s}\lambda_{k+1}}{\vartheta_{t}^{2}}\rho_{k}>-4\rho_{k}.

Finally, for
T_{3}
and
T_{4}
, by Lemma
5.2
, the bounds
\widetilde{\lambda}_{s}\leq\frac{3}{2}\lambda_{s}
and

\widetilde{\lambda}_{s}\geq\vartheta_{t}-\rho_{k}\geq\frac{99}{100}\vartheta_{t}\geq\frac{99}{100}\lambda_{k},

we have

\displaystyle|T_{3}|+|T_{4}|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\lambda_{j}}{\widetilde{\lambda}_{s}^{2}}\mathrm{osc}(\mathcal{R})+\widetilde{\lambda}_{s}\lambda_{j}\cdot\mathrm{osc}(\varepsilon(\widetilde{\lambda}_{s}))

\displaystyle\leq 4\frac{\lambda_{k+1}}{\lambda_{k}^{2}}\mathrm{osc}(\mathcal{R})+\frac{15\cdot 2^{4}}{36}\frac{\lambda_{k+1}}{\lambda_{k}^{2}}\mathrm{osc}(\mathcal{R})=\frac{32}{3}\frac{\lambda_{k+1}}{\lambda_{k}^{2}}\mathrm{osc}(\mathcal{R}).

Combining the above estimates, we arrive at

\displaystyle\widetilde{\lambda}_{s}\lambda_{j}\alpha_{j}(\widetilde{\lambda}_{s})\geq\delta_{k}-5\rho_{k}-\frac{32}{3}\frac{\lambda_{k+1}}{\lambda_{k}^{2}}\mathrm{osc}(\mathcal{R})\geq\frac{1}{2}\delta_{k}

by our gap assumption (
7.10
). Hence,

\Delta_{\mathcal{J}}^{\Phi}(\widetilde{\lambda}_{s})=\min_{j\in\mathcal{J}}\big|\widetilde{\lambda}_{s}\lambda_{j}\alpha_{j}(\widetilde{\lambda}_{s})\big|\geq\frac{1}{2}\delta_{k}.

Furthermore, (
7.5
) in Proposition
7.2
holds. To see this, note that the right-hand side of (
7.2
)

\displaystyle\frac{2\lambda_{k+1}}{\widetilde{\lambda}_{s}^{2}}\|\mathrm{Off}(\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{J}\mathcal{J}})\|+\frac{13\lambda_{k+1}R_{\max}}{\widetilde{\lambda}_{s}^{4}}\mathrm{osc}(\mathcal{R})

\displaystyle\leq 2\left(\frac{100}{99}\right)^{4}\frac{\lambda_{k+1}}{\lambda_{k}^{2}}\mathrm{osc}(\mathcal{R})+\frac{13}{36}\left(\frac{100}{99}\right)^{4}\frac{\lambda_{k+1}}{\lambda_{k}^{2}}\mathrm{osc}(\mathcal{R})

\displaystyle<3\frac{\lambda_{k+1}}{\lambda_{k}^{2}}\mathrm{osc}(\mathcal{R})<\frac{1}{2}\delta_{k}\leq\Delta_{\mathcal{J}}^{\Phi}(\widetilde{\lambda}_{s}).

We continue from the bound established in Proposition
7.2
. On the event
\mathcal{E}_{D}
, substituting the uniform local law bound
\|U^{\mathrm{T}}\Xi(\widetilde{\lambda}_{s})U\|\leq M/\widetilde{\lambda}_{s}^{2}
and using
\widetilde{\lambda}_{s}\geq\frac{99}{100}\lambda_{k}
,

\displaystyle\|U_{J}^{\mathrm{T}}\widetilde{u}_{s}\|\leq

\displaystyle\frac{4}{\Delta_{\mathcal{J}}^{\Phi}(\widetilde{\lambda}_{s})}\left(\frac{\|\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{J}\mathcal{I}}\|}{\widetilde{\lambda}_{s}}+\frac{13}{2}\frac{R_{\max}}{\widetilde{\lambda}_{s}^{2}}\mathrm{osc}(\mathcal{R})+\widetilde{\lambda}_{s}^{2}\|U^{\mathrm{T}}\Xi(\widetilde{\lambda}_{s})U\|\right)

\displaystyle+2\sqrt{3}\left(\frac{\|\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{N}\mathcal{K}}\|}{\widetilde{\lambda}_{s}^{2}}+\frac{13}{2}\frac{R_{\max}}{\widetilde{\lambda}_{s}^{4}}\mathrm{osc}(\mathcal{R})+\widetilde{\lambda}_{s}\|U^{\mathrm{T}}\Xi(\widetilde{\lambda}_{s})U\|\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{8}{\delta_{k}}\left(\frac{100}{99}\frac{\|\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{J}\mathcal{I}}\|}{\lambda_{k}}+\frac{13}{2}\left(\frac{100}{99}\right)\frac{\mathrm{osc}(\mathcal{R})R_{\max}}{\lambda_{k}^{2}}+M\right)

\displaystyle+2\sqrt{3}\left(\left(\frac{100}{99}\right)^{2}\frac{\|\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{N}\mathcal{K}}\|}{{\lambda}_{k}^{2}}+\frac{13}{2}\left(\frac{100}{99}\right)\frac{\mathrm{osc}(\mathcal{R})R_{\max}}{{\lambda}_{k}^{2}}+\frac{100}{99}\frac{M}{\lambda_{k}}\right)

Now the bound above simplifies to

\displaystyle\|U_{J}^{\mathrm{T}}\widetilde{u}_{s}\|<\frac{10}{\delta_{k}\lambda_{k}}\left(\|\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{J}\mathcal{I}}\|+8\frac{R_{\max}}{\lambda_{k}^{2}}\mathrm{osc}(\mathcal{R})\right)+3\frac{\|\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{N}\mathcal{K}}\|}{\lambda_{k}^{2}}+10\frac{M}{\delta_{k}}.

Finally, we establish the bound

\displaystyle\|U_{J}^{\mathrm{T}}\widetilde{U}_{k}\|_{F}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\sum_{s=1}^{k}\|U_{J}^{\mathrm{T}}\widetilde{u}_{s}\|^{2}}

(7.14)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{10\sqrt{k}}{\delta_{k}\lambda_{k}}\left(\|\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{J}\mathcal{I}}\|+8\frac{R_{\max}}{\lambda_{k}^{2}}\mathrm{osc}(\mathcal{R})\right)+3\sqrt{k}\frac{\|\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{N}\mathcal{K}}\|}{\lambda_{k}^{2}}+10\sqrt{k}\frac{M}{\delta_{k}}.

(7.15)

Combining (
7.9
), for any
k\in[r_{+}]
, we always have

\displaystyle\|U_{J}^{\mathrm{T}}\widetilde{U}_{k}\|_{F}\leq\mathcal{B}_{k}+10\sqrt{k}\frac{M}{\delta_{k}},

(7.16)

where

\mathcal{B}_{k}:=\frac{10\sqrt{k}}{\delta_{k}\lambda_{k}}\left(\|\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{J}\mathcal{I}}\|+8\frac{R_{\max}}{\lambda_{k}^{2}}\mathrm{osc}(\mathcal{R})\right)+4\sqrt{k}\frac{\|\mathcal{V}_{\mathcal{N}\mathcal{K}}\|}{\lambda_{k}^{2}}.

Consequently, by (
7.2
),

\displaystyle\|\sin\angle(U_{k},\widetilde{U}_{k})\|\leq\mathcal{B}_{k}+10\sqrt{k}\frac{M}{\delta_{k}}+2\frac{\|E\|}{\lambda_{k}}.

From (
7.1
), using (
7.16
), on the event
\mathcal{E}_{D}
, we also have

\displaystyle\|\widetilde{U}_{k}-P_{U_{k}}\widetilde{U}_{k}\|_{2,\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\|U\|_{2,\infty}\cdot\|U_{J}^{\mathrm{T}}\widetilde{U}_{k}\|_{F}+\frac{13}{2}\sqrt{k}\|U\|_{2,\infty}\frac{R_{\max}}{\widetilde{\lambda}_{k}^{2}}+4\sqrt{\sum_{s=1}^{k}\widetilde{\lambda}_{s}^{2}\max_{1\leq i\leq n}\left\|e_{i}^{\mathrm{T}}Q\Xi(z)U\right\|^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\|U\|_{2,\infty}\left(\mathcal{B}_{k}+10\sqrt{k}\frac{M}{\delta_{k}}+8\frac{{R}_{\max}}{\lambda_{k}^{2}}\right)+4M\sqrt{\sum_{s=1}^{k}\frac{1}{\widetilde{\lambda}_{s}^{2}}}

\displaystyle\leq\|U\|_{2,\infty}\left(\mathcal{B}_{k}+10\sqrt{k}\frac{M}{\delta_{k}}+8\frac{{R}_{\max}}{\lambda_{k}^{2}}\right)+5\sqrt{k}\frac{M}{\lambda_{k}}.

Now, from (
7.3
), we obtain that

\displaystyle\min_{O\in\mathbb{O}(k)}\|\widetilde{U}_{k}-U_{k}O\|_{2,\infty}

\displaystyle\leq\|\widetilde{U}_{k}-P_{U_{k}}\widetilde{U}_{k}\|_{2,\infty}+\|U_{k}\|_{2,\infty}\|\sin\angle(U_{k},\widetilde{U}_{k})\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 4\|U\|_{2,\infty}\left(\mathcal{B}_{k}+20\sqrt{k}\frac{M}{\delta_{k}}+30\frac{R_{\max}}{\lambda_{k}^{2}}\right)+120\sqrt{k}\frac{M}{\lambda_{k}}

by our assumptions on
\lambda_{k}
and
\delta_{k}
.

8
Proof of the isotropic local law: Theorem
4

Let
z\in\mathbb{C}
satisfy
|z|\geq 6\sqrt{R_{\max}}
. Recall that

G(z)=(zI-E)^{-1}.

Denote

H\equiv H(z)=\mathrm{diag}(h_{1}(z),\dots,h_{n}(z)),\qquad h_{i}\equiv h_{i}(z)=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\sigma_{ij}^{2}\phi_{j}(z).

(8.1)

Then the QVE can be written as

\Phi(z)=(zI-H)^{-1}.

Since
(zI-E)G=I
and
(zI-H)\Phi=I
, subtracting these identities, we have

z(G-\Phi)=EG-H\Phi=(E-H)G+H(G-\Phi).

Rearranging the terms, we further obtain
(zI-H)(G-\Phi)=(E-H)G
. Hence, for deterministic unit vectors
v,w\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
,

v^{\mathrm{T}}(G-\Phi)w=v^{\mathrm{T}}(z-H)^{-1}(E-H)Gw.

(8.2)

Set

\widehat{v}^{\mathrm{T}}:=v^{\mathrm{T}}(z-H)^{-1}.

Then

v^{\mathrm{T}}(G-\Phi)w=\widehat{v}^{\mathrm{T}}(E-H)Gw.

We also define

L\equiv L(z)=\mathrm{diag}(l_{1}(z),\dots,l_{n}(z)),\qquad l_{i}\equiv l_{i}(z)=\sum_{j=1}^{n}\sigma_{ij}^{2}G_{jj}(z).

(8.3)

Then (
8.2
) gives the decomposition

v^{\mathrm{T}}(G-\Phi)w=\widehat{v}^{\mathrm{T}}(E-L)Gw+\widehat{v}^{\mathrm{T}}(L-H)Gw.

(8.4)

We start with a bound on
\|\widehat{v}\|
. By Lemma
5.1
,

\|H\|\leq R_{\max}\max_{i}|\phi_{i}(z)|\leq\frac{3R_{\max}}{2|z|}\leq\frac{|z|}{4},

where the last inequality follows from
|z|^{2}\geq 36R_{\max}
. Hence

\|\widehat{v}\|\leq\|(zI-H)^{-1}\|\leq\frac{1}{|z|-\|H\|}\leq\frac{4}{3|z|}.

(8.5)

With this
\|\widehat{v}\|
bound, the control of the first error term in (
8.4
) reduces to the following proposition, which we prove in Appendix
C
. The proof follows the cumulant expansion strategy of He, Knowles and Rosenthal
[
57
]
, which compares the random resolvent with its deterministic self-consistent approximation. Our application is simpler in two ways. First, we work only in the large-
z
outlier regime, where resolvent derivatives have elementary bounds. Second, we need only a second-order cumulant expansion with a controlled third-order remainder. The main challenge is handling highly inhomogeneous and sparse variance profiles.

P
ROPOSITION
8.1
.

Fix
D>0
. Let
v,w\in\mathbb{R}^{n}
be deterministic unit vectors, and let

z\in\mathbb{C}_{+}
satisfy
|z|\geq 6\sqrt{R_{\max}}
. Under
Assumption
1.1
and one of
Assumptions
1.2
and
1.3
, with probability
at least
1-n^{-D-4}
,

\left|v^{\mathrm{T}}(E-L)Gw\right|\leq\frac{\mathfrak{m}_{D}}{|z|}+Cn^{-500}.

(8.6)

Here, the
Cn^{-500}
term appears only in the general sub-Gaussian regime; in
the bounded sparse-entry regime, it may be omitted.

The second error term in (
8.4
) is controlled using Proposition
8.1
and a deterministic stability argument for the
diagonal self-consistent equation. Its proof is given in Appendix
D
.

P
ROPOSITION
8.2
.

Fix
D>0
. Under the assumption of Propositon
8.1
, with probability at least
1-n^{-D-4}
:

\displaystyle\left|{v}^{\mathrm{T}}(L-H)Gw\right|\leq C\left(\frac{\mathfrak{m}_{D}}{|z|}+n^{-500}\right).

In the bounded sparse-entry regime, the
n^{-500}
term may be omitted.

Proof of Theorem
4
.

We use the decomposition (
8.4
). For the first error term in (
8.4
),
Proposition
8.1
and (
8.5
) yield

\left|\widehat{v}^{\mathrm{T}}(E-L)Gw\right|\leq\|\widehat{v}\|\left(\frac{\mathfrak{m}_{D}}{|z|}+Cn^{-500}\right)\leq C\frac{\mathfrak{m}_{D}}{|z|^{2}}+C\frac{n^{-500}}{|z|}.

(8.7)

Similarly, for the second error term in (
8.4
), from Proposition
8.2
and (
8.5
), we get

\left|\widehat{v}^{\mathrm{T}}(L-H)Gw\right|\leq C\frac{\mathfrak{m}_{D}}{|z|^{2}}+C\frac{n^{-500}}{|z|}.

(8.8)

Combining (
8.7
) and (
8.8
